Quantum Gibbs Sampling in infinite dimensions: Generation, mixing times and circuit implementation
Simon Becker, Cambyse Rouzé, Robert Salzmann

Abstract
. We develop a rigorous and implementable framework for Gibbs sampling of infinite-dimensional quantum systems governed by unbounded Hamiltonians. Extending dissipative Gibbs samplers beyond finite dimensions raises fundamental obstacles, including ill-defined generators, the absence of spectral gaps on natural Banach spaces, and tensions between implementability and convergence guarantees. We overcome these issues by constructing KMS-symmetric quantum Markov semigroups on separable Hilbert spaces that are both well-posed and efficiently implementable on qubit hardware. Our generation theory is based on the abstract framework of Dirichlet forms, adapted here to the case of algebras of bounded operators over separable Hilbert spaces. Leveraging the spectral properties of our self-adjoint generators, we establish quantitative convergence results in trace distance, including regimes of fast thermalization. In contrast, we also identify Hamiltonians for which a naive choice of generators guaranteeing implementability generally comes at the cost of losing uniform exponential convergence guarantees for the associated evolutions, thereby establishing a strong trade-off between implementability and convergence. Our framework applies to a wide class of models—including Schrödinger operators, Gaussian systems, and Bose–Hubbard Hamiltonians—and provides a unified approach linking rigorous infinite-dimensional analysis with algorithmic Gibbs state preparation.



1.
Introduction

Davies semigroups
[
1
]
are among the most prominently used models for the thermalization of quantum systems into their Gibbs states. Formally, for a system at inverse temperature
\beta
with an associated finite-dimensional Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
and Hamiltonian
H
, and a finite set of so-called
bare jump
operators
\{A^{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}
on
\mathcal{H}
that is closed under taking adjoints, i.e.
\{A^{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}=\{(A^{\alpha})^{\dagger}\}_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}
, with trivial commutant, the generator in the Schrödinger picture is defined as

(1.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{D}}(\rho)

\displaystyle\!=\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathcal{A}\\
\omega\in B(H)\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!\Upsilon(\omega)\Big(A^{\alpha}_{\omega}\rho(A^{\alpha}_{\omega})^{\dagger}-\tfrac{1}{2}\{(A^{\alpha}_{\omega})^{\dagger}A^{\alpha}_{\omega},\rho\}\Big).

Here
B(H)=\operatorname{Sp}(H)-\operatorname{Sp}(H)
denotes the set of Bohr frequencies of
H
. Given the spectral decomposition
H=\sum_{E\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}EP_{E}
, the jump operators take the form

\displaystyle A^{\alpha}_{\omega}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}E,E^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)\\
E-E^{\prime}=\omega\end{subarray}}P_{E}\,A^{\alpha}\,P_{E^{\prime}}.

The function
\Upsilon:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
encodes the rate at which each jump occurs, and in order for the evolution to fix the Gibbs state
\sigma_{\beta}:=\frac{e^{-\beta H}}{\operatorname{Tr}(e^{-\beta H})}
, it satisfies the KMS symmetry condition
\Upsilon(-\omega)=e^{\beta\omega}\,\Upsilon(\omega)
. A well-known difficulty with Davies generators, already present in finite dimensions, is that the jump operators depend on the generally unknown spectral decomposition of
H
. This dependence renders both their circuit implementation and general proofs of convergence for the resulting dynamics particularly delicate. Recently, several alternatives to Davies generators have been proposed whose definitions avoid the spectral decomposition of
H
, making them appealing for quantum simulation tasks
[
2
,
3
,
4
]
. Previous methods generally achieved only approximate preparation of the target Gibbs state, and only under restrictive assumptions. By contrast,
[
5
]
provided the first exact sampler based on Lindbladian dynamics. This result was later complemented by simpler Lindbladian constructions
[
6
,
7
]
that require only finitely many jumps.
In
[
6
]
, the jump operators are defined as matrix-valued integrals of the form

(1.2)

\displaystyle L^{\alpha}

\displaystyle:=\int e^{itH}A^{\alpha}e^{-itH}\,{f}(t)\,dt=\sum_{E,E^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}\,\widehat{f}(E{-}E^{\prime})\,P_{E}A^{\alpha}P_{E^{\prime}}=\sum_{\nu\in B(H)}\widehat{f}(\nu)\,A^{\alpha}_{\nu},

with a smooth and sufficiently fast decaying
filter function

f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
. Note that we take the slightly unconventional definition
\widehat{f}(\nu)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)e^{i\nu t}\ dt
from
[
6
]
above. The integral formulation (
1.2
) permits implementation via oracle access to block encodings of the Hamiltonian evolution and bare jumps
A^{\alpha}
, after time-discretization. The associated Lindblad generator takes the GKLS form

(1.3)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},H}(\rho)\!

\displaystyle=\!\!-i[B,\rho]+\!\!\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\!\!\Big(L^{\alpha}\rho(L^{\alpha})^{\dagger}\!-\!\tfrac{1}{2}\{(L^{\alpha})^{\dagger}L^{\alpha},\rho\}\Big),

where the Hermitian operator
B
is carefully chosen so that the Gibbs state remains a fixed point of the evolution,
\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},H}(\sigma_{\beta})=0
.

It was recently shown that the convergence properties of the evolution generated by
\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},H}
are strictly weaker than those of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{D}}

[
8
]
, revealing a fundamental tension between
implementability
and
efficiency
. This tension becomes even more pronounced for very large systems, for which the choice of the filter function
f
becomes key
[
9
]
. This leads to the central question addressed in this paper:

Is there a viable way to reconcile
efficiency
and
implementability

for the dissipative preparation of Gibbs states of infinite-dimensional systems?

To tackle this question, we develop a rigorous framework for Gibbs samplers of infinite-dimensional quantum systems that simultaneously ensures

•

well-posedness of the dynamics, as in infinite dimensions the Lindblad generator may fail to generate a trace-preserving semigroup,

•

spectral convergence guarantees, as convergence properties change dramatically due to the absence of a spectral gap on the trace class operators in this setting, and

•

efficient implementation on finite-dimensional, qubit-base hardware.

Complementary hypercontractive criteria for relative-entropy decay within this framework are developed in
[
10
]
, with applications to Bose–Hubbard samplers with higher-order jumps and fixed-cutoff Coulomb models.

1.1.
Gibbs-preserving Markovian dynamics

It is well-known that unbounded operators that formally satisfy a GKLS-type equation on a natural domain may fail to generate legitimate quantum Markovian dynamics; for instance, the two-photon pure birth process defined with a vanishing Hamiltonian and jump operator
L=(a^{\dagger})^{2}
, where
a^{\dagger}
denotes the creation operator over
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, does not preserve the trace
[
11
, Example 3.3]
. Related pathologies were studied by Fagnola and collaborators, who established sufficient conditions for conservativity by imposing additional structural and domain assumptions on the generators
[
11
,
12
]
.
Other approaches to the generation problem include the seminal works of Davies
[
13
,
14
,
15
]
and Holevo
[
16
,
17
]
, who established abstract sufficient conditions for unbounded generators of QMSs, albeit those are often difficult to verify in concrete many-body models. More recently, simpler and more explicit sufficient conditions for generation were obtained in
[
18
]
for classes of generators whose jump operators are polynomials in creation and annihilation operators. While well suited to a broad class of continuous-variable models, these results do not apply to the type of generators (
1.3
) considered in the present work.

Main result I (Well-defined Gibbs dynamics).

Under explicit compatibility and domain assumptions on the Hamiltonian,
bare jumps and filter, we construct trace-preserving quantum Markov
semigroups on a separable Hilbert space that are KMS-symmetric and
preserve the exact Gibbs state

\sigma_{\beta}=e^{-\beta H}/\operatorname{Tr}(e^{-\beta H})

(Section
2

and
Proposition
2.8
). Under additional hypotheses,
every initial density
operator converges to
\sigma_{\beta}
in trace norm (Theorem
2.11
).

We make use of the abstract theory of KMS-symmetric quantum Markov semigroups developed in
[
19
,
20
,
21
]
in order to derive our generation theorem. Although the generator we consider is formally identical to that introduced in
[
6
]
, additional compatibility conditions relating the Hamiltonian
H
, the jump operators, and the filter function are required in order to establish the well-posedness of the master equation:
For the moment, we pick an arbitrary bounded filter function
\widehat{f}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}
which simply needs to satisfy the symmetry condition

(1.4)

\displaystyle\overline{\widehat{f}(\nu)}=\widehat{f}(-\nu)\,e^{-\beta\nu/2}.

In Proposition
2.8
, we show that these choices lead to a well-defined Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}
generating a semigroup of quantum channels over a separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
formally defined as on the right-hand side of
Equation

1.3
,
and with a coherent term
B
satisfying the following:
for any
E,E^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)
with corresponding eigenstates
|E\rangle
,
|E^{\prime}\rangle
,

(1.5)

\begin{split}\langle E^{\prime}&|B|E\rangle=\frac{i}{2}\operatorname{tanh}\Big(\tfrac{\beta(E^{\prime}-E)}{4}\Big)\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in B(H)\\
\nu_{2}-\nu_{1}=E^{\prime}-E\end{subarray}}\overline{\widehat{f}(\nu_{1})}\widehat{f}(\nu_{2})\,\langle E^{\prime}|(A^{\alpha})^{\dagger}P_{E+\nu_{2}}A^{\alpha}|E\rangle.\end{split}

At this stage, the generator
\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},H}
still seems to depend on the spectral decomposition of
H
. To circumvent this, we need representations of the jumps
L^{\alpha}
and of the coherent term
B
that are independent of the spectral decomposition of
H
. For this, we first
assume that
\widehat{f}
is the Fourier transform of an

L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
function
f
, giving the jumps the integral
formula in the first identity of
Equation

1.2
. The weighted
moment assumptions used to control truncation are stated in
Section
4.3
. Next, we find a spectrally agnostic representation of the coherent part
B
. In finite dimensions,
[
6
]
considered the drift part
G\rho+\rho\,G^{\dagger}
, with

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle:=-iB-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha}(L^{\alpha})^{\dagger}L^{\alpha}=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\sum_{\nu\in B(H)}\widehat{g}(\nu)\big((L^{\alpha})^{\dagger}L^{\alpha}\big)_{\nu},

with
\widehat{g}(\nu):=-\frac{1}{2}\big(\operatorname{tanh}(-\beta\nu/4)+1\big)
. As such, since the function
\widehat{g}
does not possess a smooth Fourier transform,
G
does not seem to admit an integral representation.
In
[
6
]
, the issue was resolved by introducing a compactly supported cut-off function
\kappa(\nu)=1
for
\nu\in[-2\|H\|,2\|H\|]
, and denoting

\widehat{g}_{\kappa}(\nu):=\widehat{g}(\nu)\,\kappa(\nu).

This way,
G
coincides with the operator defined by replacing
\widehat{g}
with
\widehat{g}_{\kappa}
. Moreover, since
\widehat{g}_{\kappa}
admits a Fourier transform
g_{\kappa}
, we can write

\displaystyle G=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g_{\kappa}(t)\,e^{iHt}\big((L^{\alpha})^{\dagger}L^{\alpha}\big)e^{-itH}\,dt.

When
H
is unbounded, a sharp cutoff function
\kappa
regularizing the function
\widehat{g}_{\kappa}
, without modifying the generator
G
, no longer exists. Moreover, imposing a cut-off would eventually lead to a loss of the Gibbs-preservation property of the evolution generated by
\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},H}
.

To resolve these conflicting constraints, we consider the following Gaussian weighted version of the generator
\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},H}
, which has already been considered in finite dimensions in
[
7
,
8
]
: for a given width
\sigma_{E}\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}(\rho)\!:=\!\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}\!e^{-\frac{(\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})^{2}}{8\sigma_{E}^{2}}}\,\overline{\widehat{f}(\nu_{1})}\widehat{f}(\nu_{2})\Big(\!\!-i[B^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}},\rho]+A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}\rho(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}\!-\!\frac{1}{2}\{(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}},\rho\}\Big),

where

\displaystyle B^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{2}\tanh(\beta(\nu_{2}-\nu_{1})/4)\,(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}.

This formal definition is shown to generate a semigroup of quantum channels fixing
\sigma_{\beta}
in
Section

4.1
. The semigroup is, in fact, KMS-symmetric with respect to
\sigma_{\beta}
, which means that for any
t
, the Heisenberg-dual, weak
∗
-continuous semigroup generated by
\mathcal{L}_{\smash{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}}^{\dagger}
, is self-adjoint with respect to the so-called KMS scalar product on the bounded operators on
\mathcal{H}

(1.6)

\displaystyle\langle\bullet,\bullet\rangle_{\sigma_{\beta}}:(X,Y)\mapsto\operatorname{Tr}\Big(\sigma_{\beta}^{\frac{1}{2}}X^{\dagger}\sigma_{\beta}^{\frac{1}{2}}Y\Big).

Moreover,
\mathcal{L}_{\smash{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}}
interpolates between the generator
\mathcal{L}_{\smash{\widehat{f},H}}
at
\sigma_{E}=\infty
and the Davies generator
\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{D}}
associated with
H
at a rate of
\Upsilon=|\widehat{f}|^{2}
at
\sigma_{E}=0
. Most importantly, the added Gaussian envelope helps in regularizing the drift: in Proposition
4.4
, we show that

(1.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}(\rho)=G_{\sigma_{E}}\,\rho+\rho\,G_{\sigma_{E}}^{\dagger}+\Phi_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}(\rho),

with

\displaystyle G_{\sigma_{E}}:=-\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}g(t)\,e^{itH}((L^{\alpha})^{\dagger}L^{\alpha})e^{-itH}dt,

with

g(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-\nu^{2}/8\sigma_{E}^{2}}}{1+e^{\beta\nu/2}}e^{-i\nu t}d\nu
,
X^{\alpha}_{s}:=e^{isH}L^{\alpha}e^{-isH}
, and a CP map

\displaystyle\Phi_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}(\rho):=\sigma_{E}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sum_{\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-2\sigma_{E}^{2}s^{2}}\,\,X_{s}^{\alpha}\rho(X_{s}^{\alpha})^{\dagger}\,ds.

Under the standing assumptions on
H
and the bare jumps, an
integrable time-domain filter with KMS-symmetric Fourier transform
therefore gives a spectral-agnostic integral representation of a
generator that exactly fixes the Gibbs state of
H
at inverse
temperature
\beta
.

1.2.
Convergence guarantees via spectral analysis

Assuming
\ker\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}=\mathbb{C}{\sigma_{\beta}}
, we argue that the evolution generated by
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
converges to its fixed point
\sigma_{\beta}
.
Given a subset
\mathscr{S}
of input quantum states, we define the mixing time by

t_{\operatorname{mix}}(\varepsilon,\mathscr{S})\!:=\!\inf\Bigl\{t\geq 0\Big|\|\rho_{t}-\sigma_{\beta}\|_{1}\leq\varepsilon\,\forall\rho\in\mathscr{S}\!\Bigr\}

where
\rho_{t}
denotes the state
e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}}(\rho)
evolved according to the semigroup generated by
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
, and
\sigma_{\beta}
is its unique stationary state.
Establishing polynomial-time convergence for Gibbs sampling dynamics is notoriously challenging, as the mixing time depends delicately on both the Hamiltonian
H
and the inverse temperature
\beta
. In
[
9
]
, it was first proved that high-temperature Gibbs states of geometrically local Hamiltonians with locally finite-dimensional constituents can be prepared in time
t_{\mathrm{mix}}=\mathcal{O}(n)
; this was later improved in
[
22
]
to the optimal bound
t_{\mathrm{mix}}=\mathcal{O}(\log n)
. These mixing results were subsequently extended to several low-temperature regimes, including spin chains
[
23
]
, certain CSS codes above critical thermodynamic temperatures
[
24
]
, and perturbations of Gaussian fermionic models
[
25
,
26
,
27
]
. In contrast, exponential lower bounds below critical temperatures were also obtained through quantum extensions of the bottleneck lemma
[
28
]
. Nevertheless, all such existing approaches currently rely fundamentally on the boundedness of the generator.

First, we contemplate the possibility of getting uniform convergence of the evolution over the entire set
\mathscr{S}\equiv\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{H})
of states: given a strongly continuous semigroup over a Banach space
\mathscr{B}
, a necessary condition for it to converge in norm is that the spectrum of its generator
A
is gapped, meaning that there exists a positive constant
\lambda_{0}>0
such that for all
\lambda\in\operatorname{Sp}(A)\backslash\{0\}
we have
\operatorname{Re}(\lambda)\leq-\lambda_{0}

[
29
, Corollary 4.1.2]
.
A natural Banach space on which one would want to study the dynamics generated by
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
is the space of trace-class operators over
\mathcal{H}
; there a spectral gap is necessary, but not sufficient, for uniform exponential convergence to
\sigma_{\beta}
. Unfortunately, this condition often fails, even in the simplest settings (see Proposition
3.1
). This first observation, in sharp contrast with the finite-dimensional setting, strongly suggests the need to restrict the set
\mathscr{S}
of input states.

Here again, the imposed KMS-symmetry condition (
1.6
) can be used to our advantage. Indeed, this condition implies that we can associate with the generator
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
a self-adjoint operator
L_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
on the Hilbert space
\mathscr{T}_{2}
of Hilbert-Schmidt operators on
\mathcal{H}
, via the defining property that for any
x\in\mathscr{T}_{2}
,

\displaystyle\sigma_{\beta}^{\frac{1}{4}}e^{tL_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}}(x)\sigma_{\beta}^{\frac{1}{4}}=e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}}(\sigma_{\beta}^{\frac{1}{4}}x\sigma_{\beta}^{\frac{1}{4}}).

By a standard use of Hölder’s inequality, we conclude that for input states of the form
\rho=\sigma_{\beta}^{\frac{1}{4}}x\sigma_{\beta}^{\frac{1}{4}}
,

\displaystyle\big\|e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}}(\rho)-\sigma_{\beta}\big\|_{1}\leq\|e^{tL_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}}(x)-\sigma_{\beta}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{2}.

Moreover, assuming
\ker L_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}=\mathbb{C}\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}
and a positive spectral gap, the above norm decays exponentially in
t
with a rate
\lambda_{2}\equiv\operatorname{gap}(L_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H})
given by the largest value for which
L_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
satisfies the condition that
\operatorname{Re}(\lambda)\leq-\lambda_{2}
for all
\lambda\in\operatorname{Sp}(L_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H})\backslash\{0\}
, so we obtain the convergence

\displaystyle\big\|e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}}(\rho)-\sigma_{\beta}\big\|_{1}\!\leq\!e^{-\lambda_{2}t}\|x-\sigma_{\beta}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{2}\leq e^{-\lambda_{2}t}\|x\|_{2},

where in the last bound we also used that
x-\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}
is orthogonal to
\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}
due to the normalization of the state
\rho
. Thus, on the set
\mathscr{S}\equiv\mathscr{S}_{2}
of states of the form
\rho={\sigma_{\beta}}^{1/4}x{\sigma_{\beta}}^{1/4}
with
x\in\mathscr{T}_{2}
, the dynamics converges exponentially fast to
\sigma_{\beta}
. Interestingly, in
Proposition

4.2
we also find that a positive spectral gap cannot decrease as
\sigma_{E}
decreases, provided the larger-width generator has kernel
\mathbb{C}\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}
(see also
[
8
]
):

\displaystyle\operatorname{gap}(L_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H})\nearrow\qquad\text{ as }\qquad\sigma_{E}\searrow,

which justifies the intuition that the Davies dynamics converges faster than that of
[
6
]
.

In
Section

3
, we show that the generator
L_{\widehat{f},H}
is gapped for various models: first in
Section

3.1
, we consider Gaussian models with quadratic Hamiltonians. All these results are valid for a Schwartz filter function
f
. For one-mode number-preserving models,
Theorem
3.2
gives a sharp criterion in terms of
the downward rates and, in particular, a gap obstruction
whenever the downward transition rates approach zero along a subsequence.
This applies, in particular, to Gaussian frequency filters and

H=N^{p}
,
p>1
, and persists under Gaussian convolution.

Main result II (A sharp criterion for thermalization rates).

For one-mode Hamiltonians
H=h(N)
, where
N
is the number operator,
with jumps
a,a^{\dagger}
, suppose that
h(n)
is eventually
non-decreasing and
h(n+s)-h(n)\geq\delta>0
for all sufficiently
large
n
, for some fixed integer
s\geq 1
.
Under the standing generation assumptions, every KMS-symmetric filter
that is real and positive on the active frequencies satisfies

\operatorname{gap}(L_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},h(N)})>0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\inf_{n\geq 1}n|\widehat{f}(h(n-1)-h(n))|^{2}>0,\qquad 0<\sigma_{E}\leq\infty.

Thus a positive gap is equivalent to a uniform positive lower bound
on the downward transition rates (Theorem
3.2

and
Corollary
4.3
).

For the polynomially damped Metropolis filters

\widehat{f}_{\eta}(\nu)=\widehat{f}_{\!\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathscr{M}$}}(\nu)/(1+\beta^{2}\nu^{2})^{\eta/2}

defined in
(
3.15
)
, the criterion becomes

\sup_{n\geq 0}\frac{(1+\beta^{2}[h(n+1)-h(n)]^{2})^{\eta}}{n+1}<\infty.

In particular, for
H=N^{p}
,
p>1
, the positive-gap range is exactly

0\leq\eta\leq[2(p-1)]^{-1}

(Corollary
3.4
). Every fixed
\eta>0

also satisfies the filter regularity assumptions of our implementation
theory (see Section
1.3
below), thus reconciling positive gaps with implementable filters.

1.3.
Efficient implementation

In order to implement the sampler generated by the unbounded Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
we consider in Section
4.3
a finite-dimensional approximation scheme which we then use to obtain an efficient circuit implementation on a qubit-based quantum computer in Section
4.4
. For that we consider for each truncation level
M\in\mathbb{N}
a finite rank projection
P_{M}
and for each
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
a projection
\pi^{\alpha}_{M}
of rank
M+1
on the factor where the jump acts, tensored with the identity on any other factors. Here, the truncated subspace
\operatorname{im}(P_{M})
serves as the
system register
of the quantum device on which we aim to implement the Gibbs sampler approximately. For instance, in many-body or multi-mode systems, the number of bare jumps
|\mathcal{A}|
is typically proportional to the number of particles or modes. Accordingly, one usually considers truncations for which the local register space is associated with the image of
\pi^{\alpha}_{M}
and the dimension of the full system register satisfies

\log\left(\dim\bigl(\operatorname{im}(P_{M})\bigr)\right)=\mathcal{O}\bigl(|\mathcal{A}|\log(M)\bigr),
although, at this stage, we are in principle free to leave the dimension of the system register unspecified.

Using the projections
\pi^{\alpha}_{M}
and
P_{M}
we can define finite-dimensional truncations of the bare jumps and the Hamiltonian as

(1.8)

\displaystyle\left(A^{\alpha}\right)^{\leq M}:=\pi^{\alpha}_{M}A^{\alpha}\pi^{\alpha}_{M}\qquad\text{and}\qquad H_{\leq M}:=P_{M}HP_{M}.

This allows us to define the finite-dimensional Lindblad generator

\mathcal{L}^{\smash{\leq M}}_{\smash{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H_{\leq M}}}

by replacing the bare jump operators and the Hamiltonian in the unbounded generator

\mathcal{L}_{\smash{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}}

with
\bigl(A^{\alpha}\bigr)^{\smash{\leq M}}
and
H_{\smash{\leq M}}
, respectively. If, in addition, the compatibility condition

\bigl(A^{\alpha}\bigr)^{\smash{\leq M}}\operatorname{im}(P_{M})\subseteq\operatorname{im}(P_{M})

is satisfied, then

\mathcal{L}^{\smash{\leq M}}_{\smash{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H_{\leq M}}}

generates a quantum Markov semigroup on the finite-dimensional system register.

In Section
4.3
, we show that this finite-dimensional generator provides a good approximation to the target generator
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
for large truncation parameter
M
. Since
\mathcal{L}_{\smash{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}}
is typically unbounded, the approximation has to be understood pointwise on suitably energy-constrained states. To make this precise, we introduce a self-adjoint, positive semidefinite energy observable
N_{\mathcal{A}}
and consider states whose expectation values with respect to suitable exponential weights in
N_{\mathcal{A}}
are finite. We then assume that both the truncated bare jumps and the truncated Hamiltonian approximate their original counterparts well on such inputs, with errors that are exponentially small in
M
up to polynomial prefactors, see (
4.11
) and (
4.28
). We further require that the Hamiltonian evolution is compatible with the same energy constraint, in the sense that it drives low-energy states into higher-energy sectors only at a controlled exponential rate, c.f. (
4.13
). As discussed in Sections
4.3.1
and
4.3.2
all of these assumptions are naturally satisfied for multi-mode bosonic systems with bare jumps being the creation and annihilation operators, certain Hamiltonians constructed as bounded degree polynomials in
a_{i}
and
a^{\dagger}_{i}
and for the choice
N_{\mathcal{A}}\equiv\sum_{i}a^{\dagger}_{i}a_{i}.

Under these assumptions, we show in Theorem
4.12
for
filters with
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
decaying sufficiently fast in time representation and input states
\rho
satisfying

(1.9)

\displaystyle\rho\leq\mathfrak{c}\,\sigma_{\beta}

for some
\mathfrak{c}\geq 1
that for evolution time
t\geq 0
and accuracy
\varepsilon>0
we can achieve
1
1

1

Here,
\mathcal{O}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}
treat all non-displayed parameters as constant and
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}
additionally supresses subdominant polylogarithmic factors in
t
or
1/\lambda_{2}
respectively.

(1.10)

\displaystyle\left\|\left(e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}}-e^{t\mathcal{L}^{\leq M}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H_{\leq M}}}\right)(\rho)\right\|_{1}\leq\varepsilon,\qquad\text{with}\qquad M=\mathcal{O}\left(\operatorname{poly}\left(\log\left(2+\frac{t\,\mathfrak{c}\,E_{\operatorname{Gibbs}}|\mathcal{A}|}{\varepsilon}\right)\right)\right).

Here,
E_{\operatorname{Gibbs}}
denotes the expectation value of the mentioned exponential energy observable involving
N_{\mathcal{A}}
with respect to the Gibbs state. In Section
4.4
, under the additional Gevrey assumption Condition
4.18
, we then combine this
result with the work of
[
4
,
7
,
5
,
6
]
, in particular
[
6
, Theorem 18]
, which provide efficient circuit implementations of such finite dimensional Lindbladian dynamics by approximating the involved time integrals via linear combinations of unitaries
[
30
]
given oracle access to the Hamiltonian evolution
e^{\smash{-isH_{\leq M}}}
and block encoding of the bare jumps of the truncated bare jumps
\left(A^{\alpha}\right)^{\smash{\leq M}}
.

Main result III (Finite-dimensional implementation, Theorem
4.23
, Corollary
4.25
).

Provided a state preparation circuit for a finite-register approximation
\rho_{M}
of the input state
\rho
in (
1.9
), as specified in (
4.51
),

e^{\smash{t\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}}}(\rho)
can be prepared on the finite dimensional system register within
\varepsilon
-trace distance with total Hamiltonian simulation time corresponding to
H_{\leq M}
of order

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\,(1+t)\,\operatorname{poly}\left(|\mathcal{A}|\,,\,\log\left(2+\frac{\mathfrak{c}\,E_{\operatorname{Gibbs}}}{\varepsilon}\right)\right)\right).

Therefore, assuming
\ker L_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}=\mathbb{C}\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}
and positivity of spectral gap,
\lambda_{2}\equiv\operatorname{gap}(L_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H})>0,
the Gibbs state of the Hamiltonian
H
can be prepared via a finite dimensional circuit with Hamiltonian simulation time corresponding to
H_{\leq M}
of order

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\left(1+\frac{1}{\lambda_{2}}\right)\operatorname{poly}\left(|\mathcal{A}|\,,\,\log\left(2+\frac{\mathfrak{c}E_{\operatorname{Gibbs}}}{\varepsilon}\right)\right)\right).

For the polynomially damped filters
\widehat{f}_{\eta}
,

\eta>0
, Lemma
3.5
provides the
weighted time integrability required for truncation, and
Lemma
4.19
verifies the Gevrey
assumption used for circuit implementation. Thus the same
implementation results apply directly to these filters. In the
one-mode setting, Corollary
3.4
and its
extension in Corollary
4.3

characterize exactly which exponents preserve a positive gap.

For many-body continuous-variable quantum systems of interest, both the quantity
\mathfrak{c}
in (
1.9
) and the Gibbs energy
E_{\operatorname{Gibbs}}
typically scale exponentially with the number of particles or modes, much like the partition function of the Gibbs state. Owing to the logarithmic dependence of the above complexity bounds on these quantities, which stems from the exponential energy constraint underlying the finite-dimensional approximation scheme, the resulting implementations of the Lindblad dynamics and Gibbs state preparation remain efficient, with resource requirements that scale polynomially in the number of particles or modes.
This statement assumes polynomial bounds on the remaining constants and oracle costs.

.
AI-assisted tools were used only for light language editing in the first version of this article posted on arXiv. 2.
KMS-symmetric generators in infinite dimensions

In this section, we construct a family of quantum Gibbs samplers for infinite-dimensional quantum systems. Given a densely defined self-adjoint operator
(H,D(H))
on a separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
with
H\geq-h_{0}I
,
h_{0}\geq 0
, and inverse temperature
\beta>0
, we aim to prepare the corresponding Gibbs state of the form

\displaystyle\sigma_{\beta}:=\frac{e^{-\beta H}}{\mathcal{Z}(\beta)},\qquad\text{ with }\qquad\mathcal{Z}(\beta):=\operatorname{Tr}(e^{-\beta H})<\infty,

for Hamiltonians satisfying the Gibbs hypothesis
\mathcal{Z}(b)<\infty
for every
b>0
.
We recall that this hypothesis directly implies, in infinite dimension, that the spectrum is unbounded above, that the spectrum is discrete, and that the energy levels cannot become
too dense
with increasing energy values. In contrast to the finite-dimensional setting, where generators of quantum dynamical semigroups have long been fully characterized
[
31
,
32
]
, extensions to unbounded jumps are more intricate. Here, we leverage a certain detailed balance condition that will allow us to define our evolutions following the abstract theory of KMS-symmetric quantum Markov semigroups developed in
[
19
,
20
,
21
,
33
]
.

We consider a finite set
\{A^{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}
of closed, densely defined jump operators
A^{\alpha}
, each on its respective domain
D(A^{\alpha})
, whose family is invariant under taking adjoints, i.e.
\{A^{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}=\{(A^{\alpha})^{\dagger}\}_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}
. We assume that a common test domain
D\subseteq\bigcap_{\alpha}D(A^{\alpha})
includes all the eigenstates of
H
; equality of the closed operators’ domains is not required, and we require the following condition throughout the paper

Condition A
.

There exist some moments
0\leq\gamma,\mu
, with
\gamma\leq\mu
, as well as a constant
C>0
such that, denoting
\widetilde{H}:=H+(h_{0}+1)I
,

(2.1)

\displaystyle\|A^{\alpha}\widetilde{H}^{-\gamma}\|,\quad\|\widetilde{H}^{\gamma}\,A^{\alpha}\widetilde{H}^{-\mu}\|\leq C.

Next, we consider a bounded function
\widehat{f}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}
with

(2.2)

\displaystyle\overline{\widehat{f}(\nu)}=\widehat{f}(-\nu)\,e^{-\beta\nu/2}\qquad\forall\nu\in\mathbb{R}.

By (
2.2
) and Condition
A
,
\sup_{\nu}e^{s\beta\nu}|\widehat{f}(\nu)|\leq\|\widehat{f}\|
for every
0\leq s\leq 1/2
.

Writing the spectral and eigenvalue decompositions of
H
as
H=\sum_{E\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}EP_{E}=\sum_{i}E_{i}|E_{i}\rangle\langle E_{i}|
, with
E_{0}\leq E_{1}\leq\cdots
, where
\{\ket{E_{i}}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
denotes the energy eigenbasis of
H
by slight abuse of notation (
\ket{E_{i}}\neq\ket{E_{i+1}}
, even if
E_{i}=E_{i+1}
), we will extensively make use of the subspaces

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}:=\operatorname{span}\{\ket{E_{i}}\}_{E_{i}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}\,,\qquad\mathscr{F}:=\operatorname{span}\{|E_{i}\rangle\langle E_{j}|\}_{E_{i},E_{j}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}

of the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, resp. of the space
\mathscr{T}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
of trace-class operators over
\mathcal{H}
.
Both spans are algebraic. Thus
\mathcal{F}

consists of finite linear combinations of energy eigenvectors,
whereas
\mathscr{F}
consists of operators with finitely many
nonzero matrix entries in this eigenbasis.
The next claim is standard.

Claim 2.0.1
.

The space
\mathcal{F}
is dense in
\mathcal{H}
, while
\mathscr{F}
is dense in
\mathscr{T}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
.

For Schrödinger operators, we then have the following theorem that shows that, for the set of bare jumps given by the creation and annihilation operators, i.e.,
\{A^{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\equiv\{a_{j},a^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m},
Condition A is satisfied under very general assumptions.
A different perspective from our Dirichlet form approach, by directly verifying Davies’ conditions to obtain a semigroup in the space of trace-class operators, has been pursued in
[
34
]
.

Theorem 2.1
.

We consider the Schrödinger operator
H=-\Delta+V.

We write
\langle x\rangle:=(1+|x|^{2})^{1/2}
for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Let
V
be real-valued and satisfy

•

V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
V(x)\geq c\langle x\rangle^{r}-c_{0},
with
c>0,c_{0}\geq 0,r\geq 1,

as well as

|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}V(x)|\leq C_{\alpha}\langle x\rangle^{r-|\alpha|},\qquad\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d}.

Choose
\lambda>c_{0}+1
and set

\widetilde{H}:=H+\lambda.

Let
a_{j},a_{j}^{\dagger}
be the annihilation and creation operators.
Then for every
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

\widetilde{H}^{n}a_{j}\widetilde{H}^{-n-1},\widetilde{H}^{n}a_{j}^{\dagger}\widetilde{H}^{-n-1}\in\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),

i.e.
\gamma=1,\mu=2
are admissible in Condition A for the set of bare jumps being
\{A^{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\equiv\{a_{j},a^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{d}.

•

V=\nu|x|^{2}+W
for
\nu>0
with
W\in L^{p_{d}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})+\langle x\rangle^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\text{ with }\alpha<2
, where
p_{d}=\max\{2,d/2\}
for
d\neq 4
and
p_{4}>2
is fixed, then
\gamma=1/2
and
\mu=1
are admissible in Condition A for the set of bare jumps being
\{A^{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\equiv\{a_{j},a^{\dagger}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{d}.

We stress that the first case includes trapping potentials to very high order and the second case includes singular potentials such as three-dimensional Coulomb potentials .
The proof of this theorem is given in Appendix
A
.

2.1.
Dirichlet forms and Gibbs generators on Hilbert-Schmidt operators

Next, for any Bohr frequency
\nu\in B(H)=\operatorname{Sp}(H)-\operatorname{Sp}(H)
and label
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
, we introduce the energy jump operators
(A^{\alpha}_{\nu},\mathcal{F})
by

\displaystyle A^{\alpha}_{\nu}\ket{E_{i}}:=P_{E_{i}+\nu}A^{\alpha}\ket{E_{i}}.

Above, we also write
P_{E}=0
whenever
E
is not an eigenvalue of
H
.
With slight abuse of notation, we also denote
(A^{\alpha}_{\nu})^{\dagger}=\big((A^{\alpha})^{\dagger}\big)_{-\nu}
, where we recall that
\{A^{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}
is invariant under adjoints. Similarly, we formally define the operators
(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}
on
\mathcal{F}
, whose actions on an energy eigenstate
\ket{E_{i}}
are

\displaystyle(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}\ket{E_{i}}=P_{E_{i}+\nu_{2}-\nu_{1}}(A^{\alpha})^{\dagger}P_{E_{i}+\nu_{2}}A^{\alpha}\ket{E_{i}}.

Next, we formally introduce the operator

(2.3)

\displaystyle L_{\widehat{f},H}(\lambda x+\mu\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}})\!:=-\lambda\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathcal{A}\\
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in B(H)\end{subarray}}\frac{\overline{\widehat{f}(\nu_{1})}\widehat{f}(\nu_{2})\,e^{\beta(\nu_{1}+\nu_{2})/4}}{2\cosh((\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})\beta/4)}\,(\delta^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}\delta^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}(x)

where

(2.4)

\delta^{\alpha}_{\nu}(x):=e^{-\frac{\beta\nu}{4}}A^{\alpha}_{\nu}x-e^{\frac{\beta\nu}{4}}xA^{\alpha}_{\nu}

and
(\delta_{\nu}^{\alpha})^{\dagger}
denotes the formal adjoint of
\delta^{\alpha}_{\nu}
in the Hilbert space
\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
of Schatten-2 operators over
\mathcal{H}
endowed with the Hilbert-Schmidt inner product
\langle A,B\rangle:=\operatorname{Tr}(A^{\dagger}B)
, i.e.

\displaystyle(\delta^{\alpha}_{\nu})^{\dagger}(x)=e^{-\frac{\beta\nu}{4}}(A^{\alpha}_{\nu})^{\dagger}x-e^{\frac{\beta\nu}{4}}x(A^{\alpha}_{\nu})^{\dagger}.

Lemma 2.2
.

Under
Condition

A
, the expression (
2.3
) defines a densely defined, negative semidefinite symmetric operator
(L_{\widehat{f},H},\operatorname{span}(\mathscr{F}\cup\{\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}\}))
over
\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
, with
\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}\in\operatorname{Ker}(L_{\widehat{f},H})
.

Proof.

We consider
x=|E_{i}\rangle\langle E_{j}|
,

\displaystyle\|(\delta^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}\delta^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}x\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\!e^{-\frac{\beta(\nu_{1}+\nu_{2})}{4}}\|(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}x\|_{2}+e^{\frac{\beta(\nu_{1}+\nu_{2})}{4}}\|xA^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}\|_{2}

(2.5)

\displaystyle\quad+e^{\frac{\beta(\nu_{2}-\nu_{1})}{4}}\|(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}xA^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}\|_{2}+e^{\frac{\beta(\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})}{4}}\|A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}x(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}\|_{2}.

We treat each term in (
2.5
) one by one: For the first one we see

\displaystyle\sum_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}\frac{|\widehat{f}(\nu_{1})\widehat{f}(\nu_{2})|\,}{2\cosh((\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})\beta/4)}\|(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}|E_{i}\rangle\langle E_{j}|\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{E^{\prime},E\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}\frac{|\widehat{f}(E^{\prime}-E)\widehat{f}(E^{\prime}-E_{i})|\,}{2\cosh((E_{i}-E)\beta/4)}\|P_{E}(A^{\alpha})^{\dagger}P_{E^{\prime}}A^{\alpha}|E_{i}\rangle\langle E_{j}|\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\widehat{f}\|_{\infty}\sum_{E,E^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}\frac{|\widehat{f}(E^{\prime}-E)|}{2\cosh((E_{i}-E)\beta/4)}\|P_{E}(A^{\alpha})^{\dagger}P_{E^{\prime}}A^{\alpha}|E_{i}\rangle\langle E_{j}|\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq e^{\beta E_{i}/4}\|\widehat{f}\|_{\infty}\|\widetilde{H}^{-\gamma}A^{\alpha}\|\|\widetilde{H}^{\gamma}A^{\alpha}\widetilde{H}^{-\mu}\|(E_{i}+1+h_{0})^{\mu}\sum_{E,E^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}|\widehat{f}(E^{\prime}-E)|e^{-\beta E/4}.

It remains to argue that the last sum above converges. Indeed, by the boundedness and KMS symmetry (
2.2
) of
\widehat{f}
in
Condition

A
:

\displaystyle\sum_{E,E^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}|\widehat{f}(E^{\prime}-E)|e^{-\beta E/4}

\displaystyle=\sum_{E,E^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}|\widehat{f}(E^{\prime}-E)|^{3/4}|\widehat{f}(E^{\prime}-E)|^{1/4}e^{\beta(E^{\prime}-E)/8}e^{\beta(E-E^{\prime})/8}e^{-\beta E/4}

\displaystyle\leq\|\widehat{f}\|\sum_{E,E^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}e^{-\beta(E+E^{\prime})/8}<\infty

where the convergence is ensured by the Gibbs hypothesis.
For the second term in (
2.5
) we see

\displaystyle\sum_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}\frac{|\widehat{f}(\nu_{1})\widehat{f}(\nu_{2})|\,}{2\cosh((\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})\beta/4)}e^{\frac{\beta(\nu_{1}+\nu_{2})}{2}}\||E_{i}\rangle\langle E_{j}|A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\widehat{f}\|^{2}\sum_{E^{\prime},E\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}\frac{e^{\frac{\beta(E_{j}-E)}{2}}}{2\cosh((E^{\prime}-E_{j})\beta/4)}\||E_{i}\rangle\langle E_{j}|A^{\alpha}P_{E}(A^{\alpha})^{\dagger}P_{E^{\prime}}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq e^{\beta E_{j}/4}\|\widehat{f}\|^{2}\|A^{\alpha}\widetilde{H}^{-\gamma}\|\|\widetilde{H}^{-\mu}A^{\alpha}\widetilde{H}^{\gamma}\|e^{\beta E_{j}/2}(E_{j}+h_{0}+1)^{\mu}\times

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(\sum_{E\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}e^{-\beta E/2}\right)\left(\sum_{E^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}e^{-\beta E^{\prime}/4}\right)<\infty\,,

Here we again used the KMS symmetry
(
2.2
), which gives

e^{\beta\nu/2}|\widehat{f}(\nu)|=|\widehat{f}(-\nu)|\leq\|\widehat{f}\|
.

For the third term in (
2.5
) we argue as

\displaystyle\sum_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}\frac{|\widehat{f}(\nu_{1})\widehat{f}(\nu_{2})|\,}{2\cosh((\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})\beta/4)}e^{\frac{\beta\nu_{2}}{2}}\|(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}\ket{E_{i}}\!\bra{E_{j}}A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq e^{\frac{\beta E_{j}}{2}}\|\widehat{f}\|^{2}_{\infty}\sum_{E,E^{\prime\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}\frac{e^{\frac{-\beta E^{\prime\prime}}{2}}}{2\cosh((E^{\prime\prime}+E_{i}-E-E_{j})\beta/4)}\|P_{E}(A^{\alpha})^{\dagger}\ket{E_{i}}\!\bra{E_{j}}A^{\alpha}P_{E^{\prime\prime}}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq e^{\frac{\beta(E_{j}+E_{i})}{4}}\|\widehat{f}\|^{2}_{\infty}\|\widetilde{H}^{-\gamma}A^{\alpha}\|^{2}(E_{i}+h_{0}+1)^{\gamma}(E_{j}+h_{0}+1)^{\gamma}\left(\sum_{E\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}e^{-\beta E/4}\right)^{2}<\infty.

Finally, for the fourth term in (
2.5
) we argue as

\displaystyle\sum_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}\frac{|\widehat{f}(\nu_{1})\widehat{f}(\nu_{2})|\,}{2\cosh((\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})\beta/4)}e^{\frac{\beta\nu_{1}}{2}}\|A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}\ket{E_{i}}\!\bra{E_{j}}(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\widehat{f}\|\sum_{E^{\prime},E^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}\frac{|\widehat{f}(E^{\prime\prime\prime}-E_{i})|\,}{2\cosh((E^{\prime}+E_{i}-E^{\prime\prime\prime}-E_{j})\beta/4)}\|P_{E^{\prime\prime\prime}}A^{\alpha}\ket{E_{i}}\!\bra{E_{j}}(A^{\alpha})^{\dagger}P_{E^{\prime}}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\widehat{f}\|e^{\beta E_{j}/4}\|A^{\alpha}\widetilde{H}^{-\gamma}\|^{2}(E_{i}+h_{0}+1)^{\gamma}(E_{j}+h_{0}+1)^{\gamma}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\sum_{E^{\prime},E^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}|\widehat{f}(E^{\prime\prime\prime}-E_{i})|e^{\beta(E^{\prime\prime\prime}-E_{i})/4}e^{-\beta E^{\prime}/4}

\displaystyle\leq\|\widehat{f}\|^{2}e^{\beta E_{j}/4}\|A^{\alpha}\widetilde{H}^{-\gamma}\|^{2}(E_{i}+h_{0}+1)^{\gamma}(E_{j}+h_{0}+1)^{\gamma}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\sum_{E^{\prime},E^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}e^{-\beta(E^{\prime\prime\prime}-E_{i})/8}e^{-\beta E^{\prime}/4}<\infty.

For the second inequality we used

(2\cosh u)^{-1}\leq e^{-u}
and

\|P_{E}A^{\alpha}\ket{E_{k}}\|\leq\|A^{\alpha}\widetilde{H}^{-\gamma}\|(E_{k}+h_{0}+1)^{\gamma}

for
k=i,j
, together with the rank-one Hilbert–Schmidt norm
identity. The first and last inequalities use the KMS symmetry
(
2.2
) and boundedness in the form

e^{s\beta\nu}|\widehat{f}(\nu)|\leq\|\widehat{f}\|

for
0\leq s\leq 1/2
, with
s=1/2
and
s=3/8
, respectively.

The finiteness of the last sum is once again a consequence of the
Gibbs hypothesis. From this we can conclude that

\displaystyle\|L_{\widehat{f},H}\ket{E_{i}}\!\bra{E_{j}}\|_{2}<\infty.

Moreover, for any
x\in\mathscr{F}
, denoting
x_{\nu}:=\widehat{f}(\nu)e^{\smash{\frac{\beta\nu}{4}}}\delta_{\nu}^{\alpha}x
and
x_{t}:=\sum_{\smash{\nu\in B(H)}}e^{\smash{it\nu}}x_{\nu}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}(x)

\displaystyle:=-\langle x,L_{\widehat{f},H}x\rangle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathcal{A}\\
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in B(H)\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{2\cosh((\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})\beta/4)}\,\langle x_{\nu_{1}},x_{\nu_{2}}\rangle

(2.6)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathcal{A}\\
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in B(H)\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\beta\cosh(2\pi t/\beta)}\,\langle e^{it\nu_{1}}x_{\nu_{1}},e^{it\nu_{2}}x_{\nu_{2}}\rangle dt=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\beta\cosh(2\pi t/\beta)}\,\|x_{t}\|_{2}^{2}\,dt,

where the above manipulations are justified by Fubini’s theorem, together with

\displaystyle\|x_{t}\|_{2}\leq\sum_{\nu}|\widehat{f}(\nu)|e^{\frac{\beta\nu}{4}}\|\delta^{\alpha}_{\nu}(x)\|_{2}<\infty,

where we used
\beta^{-1}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\cosh(2\pi t/\beta)^{-1}dt=\frac{1}{2}<\infty
together with
Condition

A
so that

(2.7)

\displaystyle\sum_{\nu}|\widehat{f}(\nu)|\,e^{\frac{\beta\nu}{4}}\,\|\delta^{\alpha}_{\nu}(x)\|_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\sum_{\nu}|\widehat{f}(\nu)|\|A^{\alpha}_{\nu}\ket{E_{i}}\bra{E_{j}}\|_{2}+\sum_{\nu}|\widehat{f}(\nu)|e^{\frac{\beta\nu}{2}}\|\ket{E_{i}}\bra{E_{j}}A^{\alpha}_{\nu}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{E}|\widehat{f}(E-E_{i})|\|P_{E}A^{\alpha}\ket{E_{i}}\bra{E_{j}}\|_{2}+\sum_{E^{\prime}}|\widehat{f}(E_{j}-E^{\prime})|e^{\frac{\beta(E_{j}-E^{\prime})}{2}}\|\ket{E_{i}}\bra{E_{j}}A^{\alpha}P_{E^{\prime}}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{E}|\widehat{f}(E-E_{i})|\|P_{E}A^{\alpha}\ket{E_{i}}\bra{E_{j}}\|_{2}+\sum_{E^{\prime}}|\widehat{f}(E^{\prime}-E_{j})|\|\ket{E_{i}}\bra{E_{j}}A^{\alpha}P_{E^{\prime}}\|_{2}

(2.8)

\displaystyle\qquad<\infty.

Thus,
L_{\widehat{f},H}
is negative semidefinite on
\mathscr{F}
. It remains to verify that for any
x\in\mathscr{F}
,
\langle\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}},L_{\widehat{f},H}x\rangle=0
. For this, it suffices to compute, for any two Bohr frequencies
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in B(H)
and
x=\ket{E_{i}}\bra{E_{j}}
, the inner products

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathcal{Z}(\beta)}\langle\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}},(\delta^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}\delta^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}(x)\rangle

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{\beta(\nu_{1}+\nu_{2})}{4}}e^{-\frac{\beta E_{j}}{2}}\bra{E_{j}}(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}\ket{E_{i}}

\displaystyle+e^{\frac{\beta(\nu_{1}+\nu_{2})}{4}}e^{-\frac{\beta E_{i}}{2}}\bra{E_{j}}A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}\ket{E_{i}}

\displaystyle-\sqrt{\mathcal{Z}(\beta)}\,e^{\frac{\beta(\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})}{4}}\bra{E_{j}}(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}\ket{E_{i}}

\displaystyle-\sqrt{\mathcal{Z}(\beta)}\,e^{\frac{\beta(\nu_{2}-\nu_{1})}{4}}\bra{E_{j}}A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}\ket{E_{i}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{E_{i}+\nu_{2},E_{j}+\nu_{1}}e^{-\frac{\beta(\nu_{1}+\nu_{2})}{4}}e^{-\frac{\beta E_{j}}{2}}\bra{E_{j}}(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}P_{E_{i}+\nu_{2}}A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}\ket{E_{i}}

\displaystyle+\delta_{E_{j}-\nu_{2},E_{i}-\nu_{1}}e^{\frac{\beta(\nu_{1}+\nu_{2})}{4}}e^{-\frac{\beta E_{i}}{2}}\bra{E_{j}}A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}P_{E_{i}-\nu_{1}}(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}\ket{E_{i}}

\displaystyle-\delta_{E_{i}+\nu_{2},E_{j}+\nu_{1}}e^{\frac{\beta(\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})}{4}}e^{-\frac{\beta(E_{i}+\nu_{2})}{2}}\bra{E_{j}}(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}P_{E_{i}+\nu_{2}}A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}\ket{E_{i}}

\displaystyle-\delta_{E_{i}-\nu_{1},E_{j}-\nu_{2}}e^{\frac{\beta(\nu_{2}-\nu_{1})}{4}}e^{\frac{-\beta(E_{i}-\nu_{1})}{2}}\bra{E_{j}}A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}P_{E_{i}-\nu_{1}}(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}\ket{E_{i}}=0.

Therefore,
\langle\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}},L_{\widehat{f},H}(x)\rangle=0
for all
x\in\mathscr{F}
by the definition of
L_{\widehat{f},H}(x)
. Thus,
L_{\widehat{f},H}
remains symmetric over
\operatorname{span}(\mathscr{F}\cup\{\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}\})
, and negativity follows directly from the observation that
\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}(\lambda x+\mu\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}})=|\lambda|^{2}\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}(x)
for all
x\in\mathscr{F}
and all
\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{C}
.
∎

In what follows, we denote by
\mathscr{F}_{\sigma_{\beta}}:=\operatorname{span}(\mathscr{F}\cup\{\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}\})
.
Equivalently,

\mathscr{F}_{\sigma_{\beta}}=\mathscr{F}+\mathbb{C}\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}
is a subspace of

\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
, whereas
\mathcal{F}
is a subspace of

\mathcal{H}
. In infinite dimension,

\mathscr{F}\subsetneq\mathscr{F}_{\sigma_{\beta}}
: every element of

\mathscr{F}
has finite rank, while
\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}
has
infinite rank. The enlarged initial domain explicitly includes
the Gibbs square root, on which the generator vanishes.

By the Friedrichs extension recalled in Lemma
2.3
, the quadratic form
\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}
over
\mathscr{F}_{\sigma_{\beta}}\times\mathscr{F}_{\sigma_{\beta}}
defined as
\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}(x,y):=-\langle x,L_{\widehat{f},H}y\rangle
induces the generator of a strongly continuous semigroup

[
35
, Lemma 10.16, Theorem 10.7]
: let
\mathcal{E}
be a quadratic form on
\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
, namely a non-negative definite, symmetric bilinear form
D(\mathcal{E})\times D(\mathcal{E})\to\mathbb{R}
, where
D(\mathcal{E})
is a dense subspace of
\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
. The form
\mathcal{E}
is said to be closed if
D(\mathcal{E})
equipped with the norm
\|x\|_{D(\mathcal{E})}:=\sqrt{\|x\|_{2}^{2}+\mathcal{E}(x)}
is a Hilbert space.
\mathcal{E}
is said to be closable if it admits a closed extension, i.e., there exists a closed quadratic form
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
such that
D(\mathcal{E})\subset D(\mathcal{E}^{\prime})
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
coincide with
\mathcal{E}
on
D(\mathcal{E})\times D(\mathcal{E})
.
In the case of a closed form
(\mathcal{E},D(\mathcal{E}))
, we extend it to a form
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}:\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathcal{H})\to\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{+\infty\}
by setting
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(x)=+\infty
whenever
x\notin D(\mathcal{E})
. In that case,
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
is lower semicontinuous on
\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathcal{H})

[
35
, Proposition 10.1]
: for any sequence
x_{n}\to x
in
\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
,

(2.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\big(\lim_{n\to\infty}x_{n}\big)\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\big(x_{n}\big).

In what follows, we do not distinguish between
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
. Finally, given a densely defined, closed, non-negative symmetric linear form
\mathcal{E}
over
\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
, a subspace
\mathscr{D}\subseteq D(\mathcal{E})
is called a form core for
\mathcal{E}
if for every
x\in D(\mathcal{E})
, there exists a sequence
(x_{n})\subseteq\mathscr{D}
such that
\|x-x_{n}\|_{D(\mathcal{E})}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.

Lemma 2.3

(Friedrichs extension)
.

Let
L
be a densely defined negative semidefinite symmetric operator and define
\mathcal{E}(x,y):=-\langle Lx,y\rangle
with
x,y\in D(L)
. Then
\mathcal{E}
is closable, and
D(L)
is a form core for its closure.
Conversely, any closed quadratic form
\mathcal{E}
admits a unique densely defined non-positive self-adjoint operator
L
on the real Hilbert space
\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
of Hermitian elements in
\mathscr{T}_{2}
, defined on the set of elements
x\in D(\mathcal{E})
for which there exists
y\in\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
such that, for all
z\in D(\mathcal{E})
,
\mathcal{E}(x,z)=-\operatorname{Tr}(zy)
. In that case,
L(x)=y
.

The above construction readily implies that
\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}
is closable. For simplicity of notation, we continue to denote its closure by
\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}
over the domain
D(\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H})\times D(\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H})
, so that
\mathscr{F}_{\sigma_{\beta}}
is a form core for
\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}
. By Friedrichs extension, it also admits a unique densely defined non-positive self-adjoint operator on
\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
, called the generator of
\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}
. Since the latter extends
L_{\widehat{f},H}
, we also identify the two by abuse of notation. In other words,
L_{\widehat{f},H}
generates a strongly continuous, symmetric semigroup of contractions over
\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathcal{H})

[
35
, Proposition 6.14]
, which we denote by
\{e^{\smash{tL_{\widehat{f},H}}}\}_{t\geq 0}
.

In the next section, we argue that the semigroup
\{e^{tL_{\widehat{f},H}}\}_{t\geq 0}
induces a semigroup of quantum channels over the trace-class operators
\mathscr{T}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
. This is achieved by leveraging the connection between semigroups of completely positive, trace preserving maps over
\mathscr{T}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
and completely Markov semigroups over
\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
: given a faithful quantum state
\omega
on
\mathcal{H}
, we say that a densely defined, closed, non-negative definite, symmetric bilinear form
\mathcal{E}
is a Dirichlet form (with respect to
\omega
) if it satisfies the following condition: for any self-adjoint
x\in D(\mathcal{E})
, denoting by
x_{+}
the positive part of
x
and
x_{\wedge}:=x-(x-\sqrt{\omega})_{+}
, it holds that
x_{+},x_{\wedge}\in D(\mathcal{E})
and
\mathcal{E}(x_{+}),\mathcal{E}(x_{\wedge})\leq\mathcal{E}(x)
.
A strongly continuous, symmetric semigroup
\{T_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
contraction is said to be Markov (with respect to
\omega
) if and only if
0\leq x\leq\sqrt{\omega}
implies that
0\leq T_{t}(x)\leq\sqrt{\omega}
for all
t\geq 0
. More generally, let
{\mathscr{M}}_{n}
denote the algebra of
n\times n
matrices acting on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. We let
\mathcal{E}^{(n)}
denote the quadratic form on
\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathbb{C}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
, where the state on
{\mathscr{M}}_{n}
is taken as the usual trace, given by
D(\mathcal{E}^{(n)}):={\mathscr{M}}_{n}\otimes D(\mathcal{E})
, with
\mathcal{E}^{(n)}(\{x_{ij}\})=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\mathcal{E}(x_{ij})
.
Then the form
\mathcal{E}
is said to be
n
-Dirichlet if the form
\mathcal{E}^{(n)}
is Dirichlet for the underlying state
\omega^{(n)}:=\tau_{n}\otimes\omega
, where
\tau_{n}
denotes the maximally mixed state in
{\mathscr{M}}_{n}
, and completely Dirichlet if it is
n
-Dirichlet for all
n
. Similarly, the semigroup
\{T_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is
n
-Markov if for all
0\leq x\leq\sqrt{\tau_{n}\otimes\omega}
,
0\leq\operatorname{id}_{n}\otimes T_{t}(x)\leq\sqrt{\tau_{n}\otimes\omega}
, and completely Markov if it is
n
-Markov for all
n
. Next, we make use of the equivalence between completely Dirichlet forms and completely Markov semigroups
[
21
, Theorem 5.7]
to prove the complete Markov property of the semigroup generated by
L_{\widehat{f},H}
:

Proposition 2.4
.

The closed quadratic form
\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}
is completely Dirichlet.
Moreover, the strongly continuous, symmetric semigroup

\{e^{\smash{tL_{\widehat{f},H}}}\}_{\smash{t\geq 0}}
is completely Markov with
respect to the state
\sigma_{\beta}
.

Proof.

From the previous discussion, it suffices to prove that the form

\mathcal{E}_{\smash{\widehat{f},H}}
is completely Dirichlet. We first prove the
claim on the form core

\mathscr{F}_{\sigma_{\beta}}

and then pass to the closure by lower
semicontinuity (cf. Equation (
2.9
)). Recall that, on the core, the generator is given by
L_{\widehat{f},H}=K+T
, where

\displaystyle K(x):={}

\displaystyle-\sum_{\alpha,\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}\frac{\overline{\widehat{f}(\nu_{1})}\widehat{f}(\nu_{2})}{2\cosh(\beta(\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})/4)}\Big[(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}x+e^{\beta(\nu_{1}+\nu_{2})/2}xA^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}\Big],

and

\displaystyle T(x):={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}\frac{\overline{\widehat{f}(\nu_{1})}\widehat{f}(\nu_{2})}{2\cosh(\beta(\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})/4)}\Big[e^{\beta\nu_{2}/2}(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}xA^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}+e^{\beta\nu_{1}/2}A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}x(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}\Big].

We now show that
T
is completely positive. Since the set of bare jumps is
closed under adjoints, for every
\alpha
there is a label
\bar{\alpha}
such
that
A^{\bar{\alpha}}=(A^{\alpha})^{\dagger}
,
(A^{\alpha}_{\nu})^{\dagger}=A^{\bar{\alpha}}_{-\nu}
.
Using this identity in the second term of
T
, and reindexing
\alpha\mapsto\bar{\alpha}
,

\nu_{1}\mapsto-\nu_{2}
,
\nu_{2}\mapsto-\nu_{1}
, we obtain

T(x)=\sum_{\alpha,\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}\frac{\overline{\widehat{f}(\nu_{1})}\widehat{f}(\nu_{2})}{2\cosh(\beta(\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})/4)}\left(e^{\beta\nu_{1}/2}+e^{\beta\nu_{2}/2}\right)(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}xA^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}.

Define
g(\nu):=e^{\beta\nu/4}\widehat{f}(\nu)
. The KMS symmetry
\overline{\widehat{f}(\nu)}=\widehat{f}(-\nu)e^{-\beta\nu/2}
implies
g(-\nu)=\overline{g(\nu)}
. Therefore,

\frac{\overline{\widehat{f}(\nu_{1})}\widehat{f}(\nu_{2})}{2\cosh(\beta(\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})/4)}\left(e^{\beta\nu_{1}/2}+e^{\beta\nu_{2}/2}\right)=\overline{g(\nu_{1})}g(\nu_{2}).

Indeed, writing
s=\frac{\beta(\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})}{4}
, one has

\displaystyle\overline{\widehat{f}(\nu_{1})}\widehat{f}(\nu_{2})\left(e^{\beta\nu_{1}/2}+e^{\beta\nu_{2}/2}\right)

\displaystyle=\overline{g(\nu_{1})}g(\nu_{2})e^{-\beta(\nu_{1}+\nu_{2})/4}\left(e^{\beta\nu_{1}/2}+e^{\beta\nu_{2}/2}\right)

\displaystyle=\overline{g(\nu_{1})}g(\nu_{2})\left(e^{s}+e^{-s}\right)=2\overline{g(\nu_{1})}g(\nu_{2})\cosh(s).

Therefore

T(x)=\sum_{\alpha,\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}\overline{g(\nu_{1})}g(\nu_{2})(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}xA^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}.

Equivalently, if
R^{\alpha}:=\sum_{\nu\in B(H)}g(\nu)A^{\alpha}_{\nu}
, then, on the core,

T(x)=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}(R^{\alpha})^{\dagger}xR^{\alpha}
. Thus
T
is completely positive. Finite spectral/frequency cutoffs and Lemma
2.2
justify the same identities on the amplified core. We now prove the Markov property. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and let

\mathbf{x}\in\mathscr{M}_{n}\otimes\mathscr{F}_{\sigma_{\beta}}
be self-adjoint. Write
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}_{+}-\mathbf{x}_{-}

for its positive and negative parts. Since

L_{\widehat{f},H}
is negative semidefinite and symmetric, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}^{(n)}(\mathbf{x})-\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}^{(n)}(\mathbf{x}_{+})={}

\displaystyle-\langle\mathbf{x}_{-},(\mathrm{id}_{n}\otimes L_{\widehat{f},H})(\mathbf{x}_{-})\rangle

\displaystyle+\langle\mathbf{x}_{+},(\mathrm{id}_{n}\otimes L_{\widehat{f},H})(\mathbf{x}_{-})\rangle

\displaystyle+\langle\mathbf{x}_{-},(\mathrm{id}_{n}\otimes L_{\widehat{f},H})(\mathbf{x}_{+})\rangle

\displaystyle\geq{}

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{x}_{+},(\mathrm{id}_{n}\otimes L_{\widehat{f},H})(\mathbf{x}_{-})\rangle

\displaystyle+\langle\mathbf{x}_{-},(\mathrm{id}_{n}\otimes L_{\widehat{f},H})(\mathbf{x}_{+})\rangle.

The following trace identities are justified first under finite cutoffs and then by Lemma
2.2
.
The contribution of
K
to the last two terms vanishes. Indeed,
K
is a
sum of left and right multiplication operators. Hence, using cyclicity of the
trace and
\mathbf{x}_{+}\mathbf{x}_{-}=\mathbf{x}_{-}\mathbf{x}_{+}=0
, we get

\langle\mathbf{x}_{+},(\mathrm{id}_{n}\otimes K)(\mathbf{x}_{-})\rangle=\langle\mathbf{x}_{-},(\mathrm{id}_{n}\otimes K)(\mathbf{x}_{+})\rangle=0.

Consequently,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}^{(n)}(\mathbf{x})-\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}^{(n)}(\mathbf{x}_{+})\geq{}

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{x}_{+},(\mathrm{id}_{n}\otimes T)(\mathbf{x}_{-})\rangle+\langle\mathbf{x}_{-},(\mathrm{id}_{n}\otimes T)(\mathbf{x}_{+})\rangle.

Since
T
is completely positive and
\mathbf{x}_{+},\mathbf{x}_{-}\geq 0
, both terms on the
right-hand side are nonnegative. Hence
\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}^{(n)}(\mathbf{x}_{+})\leq\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}^{(n)}(\mathbf{x})
. For the upper cut-off, with
\mathbf{a}=\sigma_{\beta}^{(n)/2}
and
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{a}-\mathbf{x}
, one has
\mathbf{x}_{\wedge}=\mathbf{a}-\mathbf{y}_{+}
and, since
\mathbf{a}
lies in the kernel of the form,
\mathcal{E}^{(n)}(\mathbf{x}_{\wedge})=\mathcal{E}^{(n)}(\mathbf{y}_{+})\leq\mathcal{E}^{(n)}(\mathbf{y})=\mathcal{E}^{(n)}(\mathbf{x})
. By lower semicontinuity and the form-core property both inequalities pass to the closure. Hence
\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}
is completely Dirichlet and the associated symmetric semigroup is completely Markov with respect to
\sigma_{\beta}
.
∎

2.2.
From Hilbert to Schrödinger

Next, we show how to define a semigroup of quantum channels on the space
\mathscr{T}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
of trace-class operators from the completely Markov semigroup constructed in Proposition
2.4
. We start by deriving a simple density argument: in what follows, given a faithful quantum state
\omega
on
\mathcal{H}
and two real numbers
\lambda\leq\mu
, we denote by
B_{[\lambda,\mu]}(\omega)
the set of self-adjoint, trace-class operators
x
with
\lambda\,\omega\leq x\leq\mu\,\omega
.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
\omega
be a faithful quantum state on
\mathcal{H}
. Then the set of positive semidefinite, trace-class operators
\mathscr{T}_{1}(\mathcal{H})_{+}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}_{1}(\mathcal{H})_{+}=\overline{\bigcup_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}B_{[0,\lambda]}(\omega)}^{\|\cdot\|_{1}}\,.

Proof.

Denote by
P_{K}
the projection onto the subspace corresponding to the first
K
largest eigenvalues of
\omega
. Then, for any positive semidefinite, trace-class operator
a
,
0\leq P_{K}aP_{K}\leq\|a\|P_{K}\leq\frac{\|a\|}{\lambda_{K}}\omega
, where
\lambda_{K}
denotes the
K
-th largest eigenvalue of
\omega
. Moreover, we can clearly see that
\|P_{K}aP_{K}-a\|_{1}\to 0
as
K\to\infty
. The result follows.
∎

Next, we consider the completely Dirichlet form
\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}
over
\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathcal{H})_{\mathbb{R}}
with the associated generator
L_{\widehat{f},H}
.
Using the non-commutative Radon-Nikodym theorem (see
[
36
, Lemma 2.2c]
or
[
21
, Lemma 1.5]
), for any
0\leq y\leq\sigma_{\beta}
, there exists
X\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{H})_{+}
, the set of
positive semidefinite bounded operators on
\mathcal{H}
,
with
\|X\|\leq 1
and
y=\sigma_{\beta}^{\smash{\scriptstyle 1/2}}X\sigma_{\beta}^{\smash{\scriptstyle 1/2}}
. We denote
x=\sigma_{\beta}^{\smash{\scriptstyle 1/4}}X\sigma_{\beta}^{\smash{\scriptstyle 1/4}}\in\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathcal{H})_{+}
, so that
y=\sigma_{\beta}^{\smash{1/4}}x\sigma_{\beta}^{\smash{1/4}}
. Uniqueness of
X
(and thus
x
) follows from the fact that
\sigma_{\beta}^{\smash{1/2}}X\sigma_{\beta}^{\smash{1/2}}=\sigma_{\beta}^{\smash{1/2}}Y\sigma_{\beta}^{\smash{1/2}}\Rightarrow X=Y
by the faithfulness of
\sigma_{\beta}
. Next, given the continuous embedding

\displaystyle\iota_{2}:\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathcal{H})\to\mathscr{T}_{1}(\mathcal{H})\,,\qquad\iota_{2}(x)=\sigma_{\beta}^{\frac{1}{4}}x\sigma_{\beta}^{\frac{1}{4}},

we can define induced maps on the trace-class operators as

(2.10)

\displaystyle\Phi_{t}(y)=\iota_{2}\circ e^{tL_{\widehat{f},H}}(x).

By the complete Markov property,

\displaystyle 0\leq x\leq\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}\quad\Rightarrow\quad 0\leq e^{tL_{\widehat{f},H}}(x)\leq\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}\quad\Rightarrow\quad 0\leq\Phi_{t}(y)\leq\sigma_{\beta}.

Lemma 2.6
.

The maps
\Phi_{t}
defined above can be extended by linearity to a strongly continuous semigroup of uniformly bounded, completely positive, trace preserving linear maps on
\mathscr{T}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
, preserving its self-adjoint real subspace, with
\sup_{t\geq 0}\|\Phi_{t}\|_{1\to 1}\leq 1
and
\Phi_{t}(\sigma_{\beta})=\sigma_{\beta}
for all
t\geq 0
.

Proof.

For
y=\iota_{2}(x)
, Hilbert–Schmidt symmetry and

e^{tL_{\widehat{f},H}}\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}=\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}
give

\operatorname{Tr}\bigl(\iota_{2}(e^{tL_{\widehat{f},H}}x)\bigr)=\langle\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}},e^{tL_{\widehat{f},H}}x\rangle_{2}=\langle\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}},x\rangle_{2}=\operatorname{Tr}(y).

In particular, for
0\leq y\leq\lambda\sigma_{\beta}
, positivity and
trace preservation imply
\|\Phi_{t}(y)\|_{1}=\|y\|_{1}
.
Lemma
2.5

supplies density of this order ideal in
the positive trace class.

To obtain contractivity on the full complex trace class, let
P_{K}

be a finite spectral projection of
\sigma_{\beta}
. Every positive
operator supported on
P_{K}\mathcal{H}
is dominated by a multiple
of
\sigma_{\beta}
, so
(
2.10
)
defines a positive,
trace-preserving map on this finite matrix algebra. Applying the
complete Markov property at every matrix level shows that the map
is completely positive. Its adjoint is unital and completely
positive, hence contractive in operator norm; by trace duality,

\|\Phi_{t}(y)\|_{1}\leq\|y\|_{1},\qquad y=P_{K}yP_{K}.

The maps on these nested matrix algebras agree by their defining
formula. Since
P_{K}yP_{K}\to y
in trace norm for every trace-class

y
, they extend uniquely to a completely positive, trace-preserving
contraction on
\mathscr{T}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
. Positivity also ensures
preservation of the self-adjoint real subspace.

The intertwining identity holds for every
x\in\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
:

\Phi_{t}(\iota_{2}(x))=\iota_{2}(e^{tL_{\widehat{f},H}}x).

Indeed, approximate
x
by
P_{K}xP_{K}
in Hilbert–Schmidt norm and
use the continuity of
\iota_{2}
,
e^{tL_{\widehat{f},H}}
and
\Phi_{t}
.
The semigroup property and strong continuity now follow on the
dense range of
\iota_{2}
from the Hilbert–Schmidt semigroup, and
extend to all of
\mathscr{T}_{1}
by contractivity. Finally,

\Phi_{t}(\sigma_{\beta})=\iota_{2}(e^{tL_{\widehat{f},H}}\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}})=\sigma_{\beta}.

∎

Note that Lemma
2.6
alone imply that there is a completely positive,
trace-preserving projection
\Pi
on
\mathscr{T}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
such that

\lim_{t\to\infty}\|\Phi_{t}(y)-\Pi(y)\|_{1}=0,\qquad y\in\mathscr{T}_{1}(\mathcal{H}),

whose range is exactly the stationary trace-class operators. Indeed, the
spectral theorem gives
e^{tL_{\widehat{f},H}}x\to P_{0}x
in

\mathscr{T}_{2}
, where
P_{0}
projects onto
\ker L_{\widehat{f},H}
.
On the dense range of
\iota_{2}
set

\Pi(\iota_{2}x)=\iota_{2}(P_{0}x)
. The intertwining identity and

\|\iota_{2}x\|_{1}\leq\|x\|_{2}
give convergence there; contractivity
extends both the limit and convergence to all of
\mathscr{T}_{1}
.
Complete positivity and trace preservation pass to the limit, and the
semigroup property identifies its range and shows
\Pi^{2}=\Pi
.
If the nonzero spectrum has gap
\lambda>0
, then additionally

\|\Phi_{t}(\iota_{2}x)-\Pi(\iota_{2}x)\|_{1}\leq e^{-\lambda t}\|(I-P_{0})x\|_{2}.

The same conclusions apply to the Gaussian-convoluted generators below.

Back to the generator
L_{\smash{\widehat{f},H}}
introduced in
Equation

2.3
, we aim to obtain a Lindblad form for the generator of the associated semigroup
\{\Phi_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
. For this, we formally introduce the jumps and drift operators:

(2.11)

\displaystyle L^{\alpha}:

\displaystyle=\sum_{\nu\in B(H)}\widehat{f}(\nu)A^{\alpha}_{\nu}=\sum_{E,E^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}\widehat{f}(E^{\prime}-E)P_{E^{\prime}}A^{\alpha}P_{E},\qquad\text{for }\alpha\in\mathcal{A},

\displaystyle G:

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathcal{A}\\
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in B(H)\end{subarray}}\,\frac{\overline{\widehat{f}(\nu_{1})}\widehat{f}(\nu_{2})\,e^{\frac{\beta(\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})}{4}}}{2\cosh((\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})\beta/4)}\,(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}

(2.12)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathcal{A}\\
E,E^{\prime},E^{\prime\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)\end{subarray}}\frac{\overline{\widehat{f}(E^{\prime}-E^{\prime\prime})}\widehat{f}(E^{\prime}-E)\,e^{\frac{\beta(E-E^{\prime\prime})}{4}}}{2\cosh((E-E^{\prime\prime})\beta/4)}P_{E^{\prime\prime}}\left(A^{\alpha}\right)^{\dagger}P_{E^{\prime}}A^{\alpha}P_{E}.

It is clear that these operators are well-defined on the domain
\mathcal{F}.
In practice, they can, in fact, however, directly be defined on the larger domain
D(\widetilde{H}^{\kappa})
for certain
\kappa\geq 0
, as we show in Lemma
2.7
below.
To see this, we introduce the functions
2
2

2

From
Condition

A
we know that
\widehat{f}
is uniformly bounded. In this case, the function
F_{2}
can be bounded by the expression
F_{2}(E)\lesssim F_{1}(E)\sum_{E^{\prime\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}\frac{1}{1+e^{\beta(E^{\prime\prime}-E)/2}},
, which is often easier to analyze in practice.

(2.13)

\displaystyle F_{1}(E):=\sum_{E^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}|\widehat{f}(E^{\prime}-E)|,\quad\text{and}\quad F_{2}(E):=\sum_{E^{\prime},E^{\prime\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}\frac{|\widehat{f}(E^{\prime}-E^{\prime\prime})\widehat{f}(E^{\prime}-E)|}{1+e^{\beta(E^{\prime\prime}-E)/2}}.

for
E\in\operatorname{Sp}(H).
Note that from
Condition

A
we easily see that
F_{1}(E)<\infty
for all
E\in\operatorname{Sp}(H).
In practice, these are well-behaved and growing polynomially in
E
.

Condition B
.

There exists
\kappa_{1}>\gamma
such that the following sums converge:

(2.14)

\displaystyle\sum_{E\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}F_{1}(E)(1+h_{0}+E)^{-(\kappa_{1}-\gamma)}

\displaystyle<\infty\,\text{ and }\,\sum_{E\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}F_{2}(E)\left(1+h_{0}+E\right)^{-(\kappa_{1}-\gamma)}<\infty.

Lemma 2.7
.

For
\mu\geq\gamma\geq 0
being defined in
Condition

A
,
let
\kappa_{1}>\gamma
be such that
Condition

B
holds. Then the operators
L^{\alpha}
are well-defined on the dense domain
D(\widetilde{H}^{\kappa_{1}})
for all
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
and relatively
\widetilde{H}^{\kappa_{1}}
-bounded. Furthermore,
G
is well-defined on the dense domain
D(\widetilde{H}^{\kappa_{1}+\mu-\gamma})
and relatively
\widetilde{H}^{\kappa_{1}+\mu-\gamma}
-bounded.

Proof.

As by
Condition

A
we have that
P_{E}^{\prime}A^{\alpha}P_{E}
is bounded and, in particular, well-defined on
D(\widetilde{H}^{\kappa_{1}})
, it remains to show that the sum on the right hand side of the definition
L^{\alpha}
in (
2.11
) when applied on
\ket{\psi}\in D(\widetilde{H}^{\kappa_{1}})
has finite Hilbert space norm
3
3

3

More precisely, we show absolute convergence of
\sum_{E,E^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}{H}}|\widehat{f}(E^{\prime}-E)|\|P_{E^{\prime}}A^{\alpha}P_{E}\ket{\psi}\|.
Hence, the sequence
\ket{\varphi_{\tilde{E}}}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}E,E^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)\\
E,E^{\prime}\leq\tilde{E}\end{subarray}}\widehat{f}(E^{\prime}-E)P_{E^{\prime}}A^{\alpha}P_{E}\ket{\psi}
converges in
\mathcal{H}
and we define
L^{\alpha}\ket{\psi}:=\lim_{\tilde{E}}\ket{\varphi_{\tilde{E}}}.
.
For
\ket{\psi}\in D(\widetilde{H}^{\kappa_{1}})
we see

\displaystyle\left\|L^{\alpha}\ket{\psi}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{E,E^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}{H}}|\widehat{f}(E^{\prime}-E)|\|P_{E^{\prime}}A^{\alpha}P_{E}\ket{\psi}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|A^{\alpha}\widetilde{H}^{-\gamma}\|\left\|\widetilde{H}^{\kappa_{1}}\ket{\psi}\right\|\sum_{E\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}F_{1}(E)\left(1+h_{0}+E\right)^{-(\kappa_{1}-\gamma)}\lesssim\left\|\widetilde{H}^{\kappa_{1}}\ket{\psi}\right\|

where we used
Condition

A
on the jumps
A^{\alpha}
and
Condition

B
.
We can argue similarly for the
G
operator as for
\ket{\psi}\in D(\widetilde{H}^{\kappa_{1}+\mu-\gamma})
we have

\displaystyle\|G\ket{\psi}\|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathcal{A}\\
E,E^{\prime},E^{\prime\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)\end{subarray}}\frac{|\widehat{f}(E^{\prime}-E^{\prime\prime})\widehat{f}(E^{\prime}-E)|\,e^{\frac{\beta(E-E^{\prime\prime})}{4}}}{2\cosh((E-E^{\prime\prime})\beta/4)}\left\|P_{E^{\prime\prime}}\left(A^{\alpha}\right)^{\dagger}P_{E^{\prime}}A^{\alpha}P_{E}\ket{\psi}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq|\mathcal{A}|\max_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\Bigl(\left\|\widetilde{H}^{-\gamma}A^{\alpha}\right\|\left\|\widetilde{H}^{\gamma}A^{\alpha}\widetilde{H}^{-\mu}\right\|\Bigr)\left\|\widetilde{H}^{\kappa_{1}+\mu-\gamma}\ket{\psi}\right\|\sum_{E\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}F_{2}(E)\left(1+h_{0}+E\right)^{-(\kappa_{1}-\gamma)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\widetilde{H}^{\kappa_{1}+\mu-\gamma}\ket{\psi}\right\|.

∎

Next, we provide an expression for the generator
\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},H}
of the semigroup
\{\Phi_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
on
\mathscr{F}
.

Proposition 2.8
.

The generator
\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},H}
of the semigroup
\{\Phi_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
on
\mathscr{T}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
satisfies
\mathscr{F}\subset D(\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},H})
. Moreover, given any two energy eigenstates
\ket{E_{i}},\ket{E_{j}}
, the generator
\mathcal{L}_{\smash{\widehat{f},H}}
associated with the semigroup
\{\Phi_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
evaluated at
\ket{E_{i}}\bra{E_{j}}
takes the form

(2.15)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},H}(\ket{E_{i}}\bra{E_{j}})\!=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}L^{\alpha}\ket{E_{i}}(L^{\alpha}\ket{E_{j}})^{\dagger}+G\ket{E_{i}}\bra{E_{j}}+\ket{E_{i}}(G\ket{E_{j}})^{\dagger}.

Following standard notations, we denote
\Phi_{t}:=e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},H}}
.

Proof.

In order to derive (
2.15
), it suffices to consider
y=\ket{E_{j}}\bra{E_{i}}
, for any two energies
E_{i},E_{j}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)
. It is clear that there exists a unique
x=\lambda_{i,j}y\in\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
, with
\lambda_{i,j}=\mathcal{Z}(\beta)^{1/2}e^{\smash{\beta(E_{j}+E_{i})/4}}
, such that
y=\iota_{2}(x)
. Therefore, since
x\in D(L_{\widehat{f},H})
, we have that
y\in D(\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},H})
and, by the continuity of the embedding
\iota_{2}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},H}(y)=\lim_{t\to 0}^{\mathscr{T}_{1}(\mathcal{H})}\frac{\Phi_{t}(y)-y}{t}=\iota_{2}\Big(\lim^{\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathcal{H})}_{t\to 0}\frac{e^{tL_{\widehat{f},H}}(x)-x}{t}\Big)=\iota_{2}\circ L_{\widehat{f},H}(x).

Equation (
2.15
) follows by the definition (
2.3
) of
L_{\widehat{f},H}
and direct computation: first, we directly get that

\displaystyle\iota_{2}\circ L_{\widehat{f},H}(x)=-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in B(H)\\
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}\end{subarray}}\frac{\overline{\widehat{f}(\nu_{1})}\widehat{f}(\nu_{2})e^{\frac{\beta(\nu_{1}+\nu_{2})}{4}}}{2\cosh((\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})\beta/4)}\,x^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}\equiv L_{1}+L_{2}+L_{3}+L_{4},

where each
L_{j}
is a sum corresponding to one of the four elements in the decomposition of
x^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}
below:

(2.16)

\displaystyle x^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}:=e^{-\frac{\beta\nu_{2}}{2}}(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}y+e^{\frac{\beta\nu_{2}}{2}}yA^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}-e^{-\frac{\beta\nu_{2}}{2}}A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}y(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}-e^{\frac{\beta\nu_{2}}{2}}(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}yA^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}.

Although this is obvious by construction, by an almost identical analysis to the one done in the proof
Lemma

2.2
, we can also verify that each of the four sums above defines an element in
\mathscr{T}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
by hand. Next, we consider the sums associated with the second and fourth terms in (
2.16
):

\displaystyle L_{2}=-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in B(H)\\
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}\end{subarray}}\,\frac{\overline{\widehat{f}(\nu_{1})}\widehat{f}(\nu_{2})e^{\frac{\beta(\nu_{1}+\nu_{2})}{4}}}{2\cosh((\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})\beta/4)}\,e^{\frac{\beta\nu_{2}}{2}}yA^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}.

Condition

A
implies that
\overline{\widehat{f}(-\nu_{2})}\widehat{f}(-\nu_{1})e^{\frac{-\beta(\nu_{1}+\nu_{2})}{4}}=\widehat{f}(\nu_{2})\overline{\widehat{f}(\nu_{1})}e^{\frac{\beta(\nu_{1}+\nu_{2})}{4}}
. Thus, by change of variables
\nu_{1}\leftarrow-\nu_{2}
,
\nu_{2}\leftarrow-\nu_{1}
, the invariance of
\{A^{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}
under adjoints further implies that

\displaystyle L_{2}=-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in B(H)\\
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}\end{subarray}}\,\frac{\overline{\widehat{f}(\nu_{1})}\widehat{f}(\nu_{2})e^{\frac{\beta(\nu_{1}+\nu_{2})}{4}}}{2\cosh((\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})\beta/4)}\,e^{-\frac{\beta\nu_{1}}{2}}y(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}.

Similarly, we get that

\displaystyle L_{4}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in B(H)\\
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}\end{subarray}}\frac{\overline{\widehat{f}(\nu_{1})}\widehat{f}(\nu_{2})e^{\frac{\beta(\nu_{1}+\nu_{2})}{4}}}{2\cosh((\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})\beta/4)}\,e^{-\frac{\beta\nu_{1}}{2}}A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}y(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}.

With these observations, we can rewrite the generator in the Schrödinger picture as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},H}(y)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\\
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}\end{subarray}}\overline{\widehat{f}(\nu_{1})}\widehat{f}(\nu_{2})\,\left(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}y(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}-\frac{e^{\frac{\beta(\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})}{4}}(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}y+e^{\frac{\beta(\nu_{2}-\nu_{1})}{4}}y(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}}{2\cosh((\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})\beta/4)}\right),

as claimed in (
2.15
).

∎

Our next goal is to show that the domain of
\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},H}
contains a larger set of energy constrained quantum states. For this, as well as in order to streamline the analysis of the next sections, we make use of a simple tool defined in
[
18
]
: given
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}\geq 0
, we introduce the quantum Sobolev spaces defined on

\displaystyle D(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\delta_{1},\delta_{2}}):=\Big\{\widetilde{H}^{-\delta_{1}}a\widetilde{H}^{-\delta_{2}}\,\Big|\,a\in\mathscr{T}_{1}(\mathcal{H})\Big\}\text{ with norms }\|x\|_{\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\delta_{1},\delta_{2}}}:=\|\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\delta_{1},\delta_{2}}(x)\|_{1},

where
\mathcal{W}^{\delta_{1},\delta_{2}}_{H}(x):=\widetilde{H}^{\delta_{1}}x\widetilde{H}^{\delta_{2}}
. Since the inverse
(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\delta_{1},\delta_{2}})^{-1}
is bounded,
\mathcal{W}^{\delta_{1},\delta_{2}}_{H}
is closed and
(D(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\delta_{1},\delta_{2}}),\|\cdot\|_{\smash{\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\delta_{1},\delta_{2}}}})
is a Banach space equivalent to the domain
D(\mathcal{W}^{\delta_{1},\delta_{2}}_{\smash{H}})
endowed with the graph norm of
\mathcal{W}^{\delta_{1},\delta_{2}}_{\smash{H}}

[
18
, Theorem 2.2]
. Moreover, for any
E\geq 0

\mathscr{S}_{E}(\widetilde{H}^{2\delta}):=\bigl\{\,\rho\in\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{H})\;\big|\;\operatorname{Tr}(\widetilde{H}^{2\delta}\rho)\leq E\,\bigr\}\subset\mathscr{S}(\mathcal{H})\cap D(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\delta,\delta}).

Lemma 2.9
.

For any
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}\geq 0
,
\mathscr{F}
is a
graph-norm
core for
\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\delta_{1},\delta_{2}}

on
\mathscr{T}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
.

Proof.

For any
a\in D(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\delta_{1},\delta_{2}})
, there is
x\in\mathscr{T}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
such that
a:=(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\delta_{1},\delta_{2}})^{-1}(x)
. We truncate both
x
and
a
by the projection
P_{n}
onto the subspace spanned by the eigenstates
\ket{E_{0}},\cdots,\ket{E_{n}}
:
x_{n}=P_{n}xP_{n}
and
a_{n}=P_{n}aP_{n}
. We have by construction that
a_{n}=(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\delta_{1},\delta_{2}})^{-1}(x_{n})
. By the proof of
Claim

2.0.1
, we have that
a_{n}\to a
and
x_{n}\to x
.
∎

This core property concerns the graph norm of

\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\delta_{1},\delta_{2}}
on
\mathscr{T}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
.
The form core used in the Friedrichs construction of

\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}
on
\mathscr{T}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
is

\mathscr{F}_{\sigma_{\beta}}
.
Next, for any two operators
(L_{1},D(L_{1}))
,
(L_{2},D(L_{2}))
that are relatively
\widetilde{H}^{\delta_{1}}
-bounded and relatively
\widetilde{H}^{\delta_{2}}
-bounded, the operators
L_{1}\widetilde{H}^{-\delta_{1}}
and
L_{2}\widetilde{H}^{-\delta_{2}}
can be extended into bounded operators, such that for any
x\in D(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\delta_{1},\delta_{2}})
, the trace-class operator

\displaystyle L_{1}\cdot x\cdot L_{2}^{\dagger}:=L_{1}\widetilde{H}^{-\delta_{1}}(L_{2}\widetilde{H}^{-\delta_{2}}(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\delta_{1},\delta_{2}}(x))^{\dagger})^{\dagger}

satisfies

\displaystyle\|L_{1}\cdot x\cdot L_{2}^{\dagger}\|_{1}\leq\|L_{1}\widetilde{H}^{-\delta_{1}}\|\,\|L_{2}\widetilde{H}^{-\delta_{2}}\|\,\|x\|_{\mathcal{W}^{\delta_{1},\delta_{2}}_{H}}.

In what follows, we also denote
I\cdot x\cdot L^{\dagger}_{2}\equiv x\cdot L^{\dagger}_{2}
and
L_{1}\cdot x\cdot I\equiv L_{1}\cdot x
.

Proposition 2.10
.

Under
Condition

A
and
Condition

B
, we have
D(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{1}})\cap D(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\kappa_{1}+\mu-\gamma,0})\cap D(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{0,\kappa_{1}+\mu-\gamma})\subseteq D(\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},H})
, and for any
x\in D(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{1}})\cap D(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\kappa_{1}+\mu-\gamma,0})\cap D(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{0,\kappa_{1}+\mu-\gamma})
,

(2.17)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},H}(x)=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}L^{\alpha}\cdot x\cdot(L^{\alpha})^{\dagger}+G\cdot x+x\cdot G^{\dagger}.

Proof.

We use that
\mathcal{L}_{\smash{\widehat{f},H}}
is closed in
\mathscr{T}_{\smash{1}}(\mathcal{H})
by the property of being the generator of the strongly continuous semigroup
\{\Phi_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
. Therefore, it suffices to show that for any
