x\in D(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{1}})\cap D(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\kappa_{1}+\mu-\gamma,0})\cap D(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{0,\kappa_{1}+\mu-\gamma})
there is a sequence
x_{n}\to x
in
\mathscr{T}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
and
y\in\mathscr{T}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
with
\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},H}(x_{n})\to y
in
\mathscr{T}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
. First, we observe that the expression (
2.17
) coincides with the definition (
2.15
)
on
\mathscr{F}
. Moreover, for any
x\in D(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{1}})\cap D(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\kappa_{1}+\mu-\gamma,0})\cap D(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{0,\kappa_{1}+\mu-\gamma})
,
there is a sequence
\{x_{n}\}_{n}
in
\mathscr{F}
such that
x_{n}\to x
and
\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{1}}(x_{n})\to\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{1}}(x),\,\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\kappa_{1}+\mu-\gamma,0}(x_{n})\to\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\kappa_{1}+\mu-\gamma,0}(x)
and
\mathcal{W}_{H}^{0,\kappa_{1}+\mu-\gamma}(x_{n})\to\mathcal{W}_{H}^{0,\kappa_{1}+\mu-\gamma}(x)
by the core property of
\mathscr{F}
(cf. Lemma
2.9
). Fixing
y\equiv\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}L^{\alpha}\cdot x\cdot(L^{\alpha})^{\dagger}+G\cdot x+x\cdot G^{\dagger}
, it suffices to show that for each
\alpha
,
L^{\alpha}\cdot x_{n}\cdot(L^{\alpha})^{\dagger}\to L^{\alpha}\cdot x\cdot(L^{\alpha})^{\dagger}
,
G\cdot x_{n}\to G\cdot x
and
x_{n}\cdot G^{\dagger}\to x\cdot G^{\dagger}
. This we show using Lemma
2.7
and explicitly only verify the first convergence, as the others follow similarly:

\displaystyle\|L^{\alpha}\cdot x_{n}\cdot(L^{\alpha})^{\dagger}-L^{\alpha}\cdot x\cdot(L^{\alpha})^{\dagger}\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\|L^{\alpha}\widetilde{H}^{-\kappa_{1}}(L^{\alpha}\widetilde{H}^{-\kappa_{1}}(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{1}}(x_{n}-x))^{\dagger})^{\dagger}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\|L^{\alpha}\widetilde{H}^{-\kappa_{1}}\|^{2}\,\|x_{n}-x\|_{\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{1}}}\to 0.

∎

2.3.
Uniqueness of the invariant state

In Theorem
2.11
, we give a criterion for uniqueness of the invariant Gibbs state and convergence to it in trace norm.
For that, we derive relations involving the Bohr components of the bare jumps from the vanishing of the Dirichlet form and give a condition under which these imply
\ker L_{\widehat{f},H}=\mathbb{C}\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}.

Theorem 2.11

(General uniqueness criterion)
.

Assume the generation hypotheses of Section
2
,
including the Gibbs hypothesis and Condition
A
,
and fix
\beta>0
. Suppose that
\widehat{f}(\nu)\neq 0
whenever

A_{\nu}^{\alpha}\neq 0
for some
\alpha
.
For
x\in D(\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H})
, define

B_{x}(\varphi,\psi):=\langle e^{\beta H/4}\varphi,xe^{\beta H/4}\psi\rangle,\qquad\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{F}.

If
\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}(x)=0
, then the Bohr-component relations

B_{x}((A^{\alpha}_{\nu})^{\dagger}\varphi,\psi)=B_{x}(\varphi,A^{\alpha}_{\nu}\psi),\qquad\alpha\in\mathcal{A},\quad\nu\in B(H),\quad\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{F}

hold. When
A^{\alpha}\mathcal{F}\subset\mathcal{F}
for every
\alpha
,
they also imply

(2.18)

B_{x}((A^{\alpha})^{\dagger}\varphi,\psi)=B_{x}(\varphi,A^{\alpha}\psi),\qquad\alpha\in\mathcal{A},\quad\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{F}.

Suppose further that the Bohr-component relations force
B_{x}
to be a
scalar multiple of
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
(in particular, it suffices
that the bare jumps preserve
\mathcal{F}
and that
(
2.18
) force this conclusion). Then

\ker L_{\widehat{f},H}=\{x\in D(\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}):\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}(x)=0\}=\mathbb{C}\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}.

Moreover,
\sigma_{\beta}
is the unique invariant density operator and

\lim_{t\to\infty}\|\Phi_{t}(\rho)-\sigma_{\beta}\|_{1}=0

for every density operator
\rho\in\mathscr{T}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}(x)=0
. Since

\mathscr{F}_{\sigma_{\beta}}=\mathscr{F}+\mathbb{C}\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}

is a form core, there are
x_{j}\in\mathscr{F}_{\sigma_{\beta}}
with

x_{j}\to x
in Hilbert–Schmidt norm and

\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}(x_{j})\to 0
.
For fixed
\alpha
and
u,v\in\mathcal{F}
, set

p_{j}(t):=\sum_{\nu}\widehat{f}(\nu)e^{\beta\nu/4}\langle u,\delta_{\nu}^{\alpha}(x_{j})v\rangle e^{it\nu}.

For energy eigenvectors
u,v
, the weak coefficients are

(2.19)

c_{\nu}(x):=\widehat{f}(\nu)\langle(A_{\nu}^{\alpha})^{\dagger}u,xv\rangle-e^{\beta\nu/2}\widehat{f}(\nu)\langle u,xA_{\nu}^{\alpha}v\rangle.

Orthogonality of the energy projections, Cauchy–Schwarz and KMS symmetry give

\sum_{\nu}|c_{\nu}(x)|\leq\|\widehat{f}\|_{\infty}\bigl(\|(A^{\alpha})^{\dagger}u\|\,\|xv\|+\|x^{\dagger}u\|\,\|A^{\alpha}v\|\bigr).

The same bound for
x_{j}-x
shows convergence of the coefficients in

\ell^{1}
, and hence uniform convergence of
p_{j}
to the absolutely
convergent series
p_{x}(t)=\sum_{\nu}c_{\nu}(x)e^{it\nu}
.
Finite linear combinations of eigenvectors follow by linearity.
Writing
w(t)=[\beta\cosh(2\pi t/\beta)]^{-1}
,
the form representation
(
2.6
)
gives

\int_{\mathbb{R}}w(t)|p_{j}(t)|^{2}\,dt\leq\|u\|^{2}\|v\|^{2}\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}(x_{j})\longrightarrow 0.

Since
w
is integrable and strictly positive, uniform convergence gives

p_{x}=0
. Averaging
p_{x}(t)e^{-it\nu_{0}}
over
[-T,T]
and passing
the limit through the absolutely summable series shows
c_{\nu_{0}}(x)=0
.
Nonvanishing of

\widehat{f}
on the active frequencies consequently gives

e^{-\beta\nu/4}\langle(A_{\nu}^{\alpha})^{\dagger}u,xv\rangle=e^{\beta\nu/4}\langle u,xA_{\nu}^{\alpha}v\rangle

for every
\alpha,\nu,u,v
as above.
Taking
u=e^{\beta H/4}\varphi
,
v=e^{\beta H/4}\psi
and using

A_{\nu}^{\alpha}e^{\beta H/4}=e^{-\beta\nu/4}e^{\beta H/4}A_{\nu}^{\alpha}
on
\mathcal{F}

yields the analogous relation for
B_{x}
and
A_{\nu}^{\alpha}
.
If the bare jumps preserve
\mathcal{F}
, summing the finitely many nonzero terms on each test vector proves

(
2.18
)
.

The assumed irreducibility of these form relations gives

B_{x}(\varphi,\psi)=\lambda\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle
.
Since
e^{\beta H/4}\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}
, this implies

x=\lambda e^{-\beta H/2}
by density and boundedness.
Conversely,
\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}
has zero form energy.
The kernel assertion follows from the representation theorem for
the closed nonnegative form associated with
-L_{\widehat{f},H}
.

The stationary-projection conclusion above now gives

\Phi_{t}(y)\to\operatorname{Tr}(y)\sigma_{\beta}
for every
y\in\mathscr{T}_{1}
.
Any invariant density operator therefore equals
\sigma_{\beta}
.
∎

Jumps with infinite energy columns.

If
H
has simple spectrum, each active nonzero Bohr frequency belongs
to at most one ordered pair of energies, and the graph of nonzero
bare-jump matrix elements is connected, the Bohr components are matrix
units along the edges. Their form relations make
B_{x}
diagonal with
equal diagonal entries, so Theorem
2.11
applies.
For example, take
H|n\rangle=2^{n}|n\rangle
and the bounded self-adjoint
jump
A=|v\rangle\langle v|
with
v_{n}>0
for all
n
and

\|v\|=1
. The nonzero differences
2^{m}-2^{n}
determine their ordered
pairs uniquely, and the graph is complete. Thus every bounded,
nonvanishing KMS filter gives uniqueness and trace-norm convergence,
although
A\mathcal{F}\not\subset\mathcal{F}
. The criterion and example also hold
for every finite positive Gaussian width: the weight
w*g_{\sigma_{E}}

is positive and integrable, so the same proof applies. No gap is asserted.

Corollary 2.12

(One-mode uniqueness without frequency separation)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
N=a^{\dagger}a
, and

H=h(N)+K
, where
h:\mathbb{N}_{0}\to\mathbb{R}
and

K=K^{\dagger}=1_{[0,n_{0}]}(N)K1_{[0,n_{0}]}(N)
for some
n_{0}
.
Assume the standing generation and Gibbs hypotheses for
H
and
the jumps
\{a,a^{\dagger}\}
, and suppose that
\widehat{f}
does not
vanish on their active Bohr frequencies. Then , for every inverse
temperature covered by these hypotheses,
\sigma_{\beta}
is the unique
invariant density operator and every density operator converges to it
in trace norm. In particular,

\ker L_{\widehat{f},H}=\mathbb{C}\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}
.
The same conclusions hold for the Gaussian-convoluted semigroups
of Section
4.1
, for every
0<\sigma_{E}<\infty
.

Proof.

The Gibbs hypothesis implies finite-dimensional spectral subspaces.
Since
K
acts in a finite Fock block, the finite-energy space of
H

is exactly the algebraic span of the Hermite basis
(e_{n})_{n\geq 0}
,
which is preserved by
a,a^{\dagger}
.
For a sesquilinear form
B
satisfying
(
2.18
)
, put

b_{mn}=B(e_{m},e_{n})
. The relations give

\sqrt{m+1}\,b_{m+1,n}=\sqrt{n}\,b_{m,n-1},\qquad\sqrt{m}\,b_{m-1,n}=\sqrt{n+1}\,b_{m,n+1}.

The boundary cases and iteration show that
b_{mn}=0
for
m\neq n

and
b_{nn}=b_{00}
. Hence
B
is scalar, and
Theorem
2.11

applies.
For Gaussian convolution, the Fourier weight in the form representation
is
w*g_{\sigma_{E}}
, where
g_{\sigma_{E}}
is the Gaussian probability
density in
(
4.5
)
. This weight is strictly positive
and integrable, so the finite-exponential-sum proof of that theorem
applies unchanged. The same KMS embedding then gives trace-norm
convergence.
∎

3.
Spectral gap

Now that we have rigorously constructed our semigroups of quantum channels that will serve as our Gibbs samplers, we need to develop a framework to study their convergence towards their fixed point
\sigma_{\beta}
. In this section, we utilize the spectral properties of the operator
L_{\widehat{f},H}
in order to control the mixing time

t_{\operatorname{mix}}(\varepsilon,\mathscr{S}_{D_{p}(\sigma_{\beta})})\!:=\!\inf\Bigl\{t\geq 0\Big|\|\rho_{t}-\sigma_{\beta}\|_{1}\leq\varepsilon\,\forall\rho\in\mathscr{S}_{D_{p}(\sigma_{\beta})}\!\Bigr\}

for a given subset
\mathscr{S}_{D_{p}(\sigma_{\beta})}
of input quantum states
\rho
with
\widehat{D}_{p}(\rho\|\sigma_{\beta})\leq D_{p}(\sigma_{\beta})<\infty
,
p>1
. Assuming
\ker L_{\widehat{f},H}=\mathbb{C}\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}
as in Section
1.2
, and writing
\lambda_{2}\equiv\operatorname{gap}(L_{\widehat{f},H})>0
,

\displaystyle\big\|e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},H}}(\rho)-\sigma_{\beta}\big\|_{1}\leq e^{-\lambda_{2}t+{D}_{2}(\sigma_{\beta})/2}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad t_{\operatorname{mix}}(\varepsilon,\mathscr{S}_{D_{2}(\sigma_{\beta})})\leq\frac{1}{\lambda_{2}}\Big(D_{2}(\sigma_{\beta})/2+\log\Big(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Big)\Big).

Under this simple-kernel assumption, controlling the spectral gap controls the mixing time.

3.1.
The harmonic oscillator

In order to build some intuition, we first consider the simple case of a Gaussian thermal state over a single-mode quantum bosonic system. Here,
\mathcal{H}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and we denote by
a
and
a^{\dagger}
the annihilation and creation operators, defined on a common dense domain
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
of Schwartz functions, where they satisfy the canonical commutation relation

[a,a^{\dagger}]=I.

The associated number operator is given by

(3.1)

\displaystyle N:=a^{\dagger}a=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}n\,|n\rangle\langle n|,

where
\{|n\rangle\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
denotes the Fock basis. We recall that these operators act on the Fock basis as

a|n\rangle=\sqrt{n}\,|n-1\rangle,\qquad a^{\dagger}|n\rangle=\sqrt{n+1}\,|n+1\rangle.

We start by choosing our bare jumps as
\{a,a^{\dagger}\}
and considering the Hamiltonian
H=\gamma N
with
\gamma>0
and
\widehat{f}(\gamma)\neq 0
. Then, given a function
\widehat{f}
satisfying the conditions of Section
2
, we conclude that

\displaystyle L^{+}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\nu\in B(H)}\widehat{f}(\nu)\,(a^{\dagger})_{\nu}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\widehat{f}(\gamma k)|n+k\rangle\langle n+k|a^{\dagger}|n\rangle\langle n|

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\,\widehat{f}(\gamma)\,\sqrt{n+1}|n+1\rangle\langle n|=\widehat{f}(\gamma)a^{\dagger}.

Similarly,

\displaystyle L^{-}:=\sum_{\nu\in B(H)}\widehat{f}(\nu)\,a_{\nu}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\widehat{f}(\gamma k)|n+k\rangle\langle n+k|a|n\rangle\langle n|=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\,\widehat{f}(-\gamma)\,\sqrt{n}|n-1\rangle\langle n|=\widehat{f}(-\gamma)a.

Computing the last two terms of (
2.15
) similarly, we easily derive

(3.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},\gamma N}(\rho)=

\displaystyle|\widehat{f}(\gamma)|^{2}\,\Big(a^{\dagger}\rho a-\frac{1}{2}\{aa^{\dagger},\rho\}\Big)+|\widehat{f}(-\gamma)|^{2}\,\Big(a\rho a^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\{a^{\dagger}a,\rho\}\Big)\,.

This coincides with the generator of the quantum Ornstein-Uhlenbeck (qOU) semigroup
[
37
]
, with a birth rate
\nu_{+}:=|\widehat{f}(\gamma)|^{2}
and a death rate
\nu_{-}:=|\widehat{f}(-\gamma)|^{2}
. Moreover, since
\overline{\widehat{f}(\gamma)}=\widehat{f}(-\gamma)e^{-\beta\gamma/2}
, it is clear that

\nu_{+}=e^{-\beta\gamma}\nu_{-}<\nu_{-}
.

Next, we choose
\gamma=1
and now consider the generator
L_{\widehat{f},\gamma N}
on Hilbert-Schmidt operators, as defined in Equation (
2.3
). A direct computation yields
[
37
]

(3.3)

\displaystyle L_{\widehat{f},N}(x)=

\displaystyle-\left(\frac{\nu_{-}+\nu_{+}}{2}(Nx+xN)+\nu_{+}x\right)+\sqrt{\nu_{+}\nu_{-}}(axa^{\dagger}+a^{\dagger}xa).

Cipriani, Fagnola, and Lindsay
[
37
, Thm. 7.2]
showed that the spectrum of
L_{\widehat{f},N}
is

\operatorname{Sp}(L_{\widehat{f},N})=-\left\{n\left(\frac{\nu_{-}-\nu_{+}}{2}\right)\;\middle|\;n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\right\}.

In the limiting case
\nu_{-}=\nu_{+}
, the spectrum becomes continuous and fills the half-line
(-\infty,0]
, corresponding to the so-called quantum Brownian motion
[
37
, Thm. 8.1]
. This equal-rate limit has no normalizable Gibbs state of the oscillator and is considered here as a limiting generator.

Why work in the Hilbert-Schmidt Space?

One might ask why we study
L_{\widehat{f},N}
on the Hilbert-Schmidt space instead of working with
\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},N}
directly on the set
\mathscr{T}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
of trace-class operators. The reason is that
\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},N}
lacks a spectral gap in the space of trace-class operators, as the next theorem demonstrates. We recall that, for a linear operator
(A,D(A))
on a Banach space
\mathscr{B}
, its resolvent
\rho(A)
is the set of complex numbers
\lambda
such that
A-\lambda I
has a bounded inverse
B:\mathscr{B}\to D(A)
, i.e.,
(A-\lambda I)B=I
and
B(A-\lambda I)=I_{D(A)}
. The spectrum
\operatorname{Sp}(A)
is the complementary set of
\rho(A)
in
\mathbb{C}
. The spectrum of a closed operator is closed, and that of the generator of a strongly continuous contraction semigroup is included in
\mathbb{C}_{-}
. A necessary condition for the uniform exponential convergence of the strongly continuous contraction semigroup associated with
\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},N}
is the existence of a spectral gap, namely a constant
\lambda_{0}<0
such that for all
\lambda\in\operatorname{Sp}(\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},H})\backslash\{0\}
,
\operatorname{Re}(\lambda)\leq\lambda_{0}

[
29
, Corollary 4.1.2]
.

Proposition 3.1
.

The spectrum of the OU generator
\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},N}
on the space of trace-class operators is the entire closed left complex half-plane
\mathbb{C}_{-}
.

Proof.

We recall that the set
\sigma_{\mathrm{p}}(A)
of eigenvalues
\lambda
of a linear map
(A,D(A))
is included in
\operatorname{Sp}(A)
. As a comparison with the quantum calculation below, the formal adjoint of the generator of the classical Ornstein-Uhlenbeck on
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})

G_{\mathrm{OU}}\varphi(x)=q\varphi^{\prime\prime}(x)-bx\varphi^{\prime}(x),

is

G_{\mathrm{OU}}^{\prime}\varphi(x)=q\varphi^{\prime\prime}(x)+bx\varphi^{\prime}(x)+b\varphi(x).

To find its spectrum, we solve the eigenvalue problem

G_{\mathrm{OU}}^{\prime}\varphi=\lambda\varphi\quad\Rightarrow\quad q\varphi^{\prime\prime}(x)+bx\varphi^{\prime}(x)-\mu\varphi(x)=0,

where
\mu=\lambda-b
.
Taking the Fourier transform of this equation yields

-q\xi^{2}\widehat{\varphi}(\xi)-b\left(\widehat{\varphi}(\xi)+\xi\widehat{\varphi}^{\prime}(\xi)\right)-\mu\widehat{\varphi}(\xi)=0.

Solving this differential equation in Fourier space gives

\widehat{\varphi}(\xi)=ce^{-\frac{q\xi^{2}}{2b}}|\xi|^{-\frac{\lambda}{b}},

for some
c\neq 0
. Taking the inverse Fourier transform yields

\varphi(x)=\tfrac{c}{2}\left(\tfrac{2b}{q}\right)^{\frac{b-\lambda}{2b}}\Gamma\left(\tfrac{b-\lambda}{2b}\right)\,{}_{1}F_{1}\left(\tfrac{b-\lambda}{2b},\tfrac{1}{2},-\tfrac{bx^{2}}{2q}\right),

where
F_{1}
denotes the confluent hypergeometric function. Asymptotically, we have

|\varphi(x)|=\mathcal{O}(|x|^{-1+\frac{\Re\lambda}{b}}).

Thus,
\varphi\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
for
\Re(\lambda)<0
, and the Fourier semigroup gives
\mathbb{C}_{-}\subset\operatorname{Sp}(G^{\prime}_{\operatorname{OU}})=\operatorname{Sp}(G_{\operatorname{OU}})
. Since
\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},N}
generates a contraction semigroup, we have
\operatorname{Sp}(\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},N})\subset\mathbb{C}_{-}.

Finally, it was shown in
[
37
]
that, given the multiplication operator
M_{\varphi}
defined by
(M_{\varphi}u)(x)=\varphi(x)u(x)
,
the weak
∗
Heisenberg generator
\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},N}^{\dagger}
satisfies, on smooth test functions,

G_{\mathrm{OU}}\varphi(x):=\bigl[\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},N}^{\dagger}(M_{\varphi})\bigr](x)=q\varphi^{\prime\prime}(x)-bx\varphi^{\prime}(x),

with

q:=\frac{\nu_{-}+\nu_{+}}{4}>0,\quad b:=\frac{\nu_{-}-\nu_{+}}{2}>0.

We now prove the spectral inclusion for the quantum
generator directly. Identify a sequence

p=(p_{n})_{n\geq 0}\in\ell^{1}(\mathbb{N}_{0})
with the diagonal operator

X_{p}:=\sum_{n\geq 0}p_{n}|n\rangle\langle n|,\qquad\|X_{p}\|_{1}=\|p\|_{\ell^{1}}.

For finitely supported
p
, equation (
3.2
)
gives
\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},N}(X_{p})=X_{Qp}
, where

(Qp)_{n}=\nu_{+}np_{n-1}+\nu_{-}(n+1)p_{n+1}-\bigl(\nu_{+}(n+1)+\nu_{-}n\bigr)p_{n},\qquad p_{-1}:=0.

Fix
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
with
\Re\lambda<0
, and set

d:=\nu_{-}-\nu_{+}>0
,
r:=\nu_{+}/\nu_{-}\in(0,1)
, and

s:=-\lambda/d
, so that
\Re s>0
. Define
p_{n}
by

P_{\lambda}(z):=\sum_{n\geq 0}p_{n}z^{n}=(1-z)^{s}(1-rz)^{-s-1},\qquad|z|<1,

using the analytic branches whose values at
z=0
are

1
. Differentiation gives

(1-z)(\nu_{-}-\nu_{+}z)P_{\lambda}^{\prime}(z)-\nu_{+}(1-z)P_{\lambda}(z)=\lambda P_{\lambda}(z).

Comparing coefficients shows that
Qp=\lambda p

componentwise. The binomial coefficients of
(1-z)^{s}
are

O((n+1)^{-1-\Re s})
; when
s
is a positive integer, they
vanish for large
n
. The coefficients of
(1-rz)^{-s-1}

decay exponentially since
r<1
. Their convolution therefore
satisfies

|p_{n}|\leq C_{\lambda}(n+1)^{-1-\Re s}.

In particular,
p\in\ell^{1}
,
p_{0}=1
, and

n|p_{n}|\to 0
. To obtain vectors in the generator domain without
assuming a finite first moment of
p
, truncate the sequence:

p^{(M)}_{n}:=p_{n}\mathbf{1}_{\{n\leq M\}}
.
The recurrence cancels every component of

(Q-\lambda)p^{(M)}
except those at
M
and
M+1
, giving

\|(Q-\lambda)p^{(M)}\|_{\ell^{1}}=(M+1)\bigl(\nu_{-}|p_{M+1}|+\nu_{+}|p_{M}|\bigr)\longrightarrow 0.

Thus the finite-rank operators

X_{M}:=X_{p^{(M)}}/\|p^{(M)}\|_{\ell^{1}}
belong to

D(\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},N})
, have trace norm
1
, and satisfy

\|\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},N}(X_{M})-\lambda X_{M}\|_{1}\longrightarrow 0,

since
\|p^{(M)}\|_{\ell^{1}}\to\|p\|_{\ell^{1}}>0
.
A bounded inverse of
\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},N}-\lambda I

would force
\|X_{M}\|_{1}\to 0
, a contradiction. Hence every

\Re\lambda<0
lies in the spectrum. Closedness of the spectrum
then includes the imaginary axis, while contractivity of the
semigroup gives the reverse inclusion. Consequently,

\operatorname{Sp}(\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},N})=\mathbb{C}_{-}.

∎

3.2.
A sharp criterion for one-mode downward rates

Consider a one-mode Hamiltonian

(3.4)

H=h(N)=\sum_{n\geq 0}h(n)|n\rangle\langle n|

with bare jumps
\{a,a^{\dagger}\}
, under the standing generation and
Gibbs hypotheses. Define the Gibbs weights and transition rates by

(3.5)

\displaystyle\pi_{n}

\displaystyle=Z_{\beta}^{-1}e^{-\beta h(n)},\qquad b_{n}=(n+1)|\widehat{f}(h(n+1)-h(n))|^{2},

\displaystyle d_{n}

\displaystyle=n|\widehat{f}(h(n-1)-h(n))|^{2}\quad(n\geq 1),\qquad d_{0}=0.

KMS symmetry gives
\pi_{n}b_{n}=\pi_{n+1}d_{n+1}
.

The filtered jumps are

L^{+}=a^{\dagger}\widehat{f}(h(N+1)-h(N)),\qquad L^{-}=a\widehat{f}(h(N-1)-h(N)).

Their squared moduli are diagonal in the Fock basis, so the coherent
correction vanishes. On finite Fock matrices the generator is

(3.6)

\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},h(N)}(\rho)=L^{+}\rho(L^{+})^{\dagger}+L^{-}\rho(L^{-})^{\dagger}-\tfrac{1}{2}\{(L^{+})^{\dagger}L^{+}+(L^{-})^{\dagger}L^{-},\rho\}.

This is a quantum birth–death generator, as studied in

[
38
]
.

Theorem 3.2

(Sharp downward-rate criterion)
.

Assume the standing generation and Gibbs hypotheses for
H=h(N)
,
the jumps
\{a,a^{\dagger}\}
and a KMS-symmetric filter satisfying

\widehat{f}(\nu)>0
whenever
A_{\nu}^{\alpha}\neq 0
for either bare jump.
Fix
\beta>0
.
Suppose that
h(n)
is non-decreasing for
n\geq n_{0}
and that
there are
s\in\mathbb{N}
and
\delta>0
such that

(3.7)

h(m+s)-h(m)\geq\delta,\qquad m\geq n_{0}.

Then

(3.8)

\operatorname{gap}(L_{\widehat{f},h(N)})>0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad d_{*}:=\inf_{n\geq 1}n|\widehat{f}(h(n-1)-h(n))|^{2}>0.

More precisely, if
d_{*}>0
, then

\operatorname{gap}(L_{\widehat{f},h(N)})\geq\frac{d_{*}}{4}\left(1-e^{-\beta\delta/(2s)}\right)^{2}e^{-\beta[\delta(1+n_{0}/s)+\Delta_{E}]},\qquad\Delta_{E}:=\max_{0\leq i,j\leq 2n_{0}}|h(i)-h(j)|.

Moreover,
\sigma_{\beta}
is the unique invariant density operator of the
quantum Markov semigroup generated by
\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},h(N)}
, i.e.
\ker\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},h(N)}=\mathbb{C}\sigma_{\beta}
,
or equivalently
\ker L_{\widehat{f},h(N)}=\mathbb{C}\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}.

Proof.

Corollary
2.12
gives uniqueness of the invariant
density operator and
\ker L_{\widehat{f},h(N)}=\mathbb{C}\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}
.
The convergence to the stationary projection established in
Section
2
then gives

\ker\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},h(N)}=\mathbb{C}\sigma_{\beta}
.
We therefore use the variational characterization of the spectral gap,
writing
\lambda:=\operatorname{gap}(L_{\widehat{f},h(N)})
.
We first prove necessity. Suppose that
d_{*}=0
.
Let
T_{n}=\sum_{j\geq n}\pi_{j}
and use the core vector

x_{n}=\sum_{j\geq n}\sqrt{\pi_{j}}|j\rangle\langle j|=\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}-\sum_{j<n}\sqrt{\pi_{j}}|j\rangle\langle j|.

The diagonal restriction is the reversible birth–death form, so

\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}(x_{n})=\pi_{n}d_{n}
and

\|x_{n}\|_{2}^{2}-|\langle\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}},x_{n}\rangle|^{2}=T_{n}(1-T_{n})
. Thus the spectral gap satisfies

(3.9)

0\leq\lambda\leq\frac{\pi_{n}d_{n}}{T_{n}(1-T_{n})}\leq\frac{d_{n}}{1-T_{n}}.

Strict positivity of the filter gives
d_{n}>0
for every
n\geq 1
,
so
d_{*}=0
supplies a subsequence
n\to\infty
with
d_{n}\to 0
.
Since
T_{n}\to 0
, the bound above gives
\lambda=0
.

For sufficiency, assume
d_{*}>0
.
For the proof, abbreviate

r:=e^{-\beta\delta/s},\qquad C:=e^{\beta[\delta(1+n_{0}/s)+\Delta_{E}]},\qquad K:=\frac{C}{(1-\sqrt{r})^{2}}.

Block growth and eventual monotonicity imply

h(n)-h(j)\geq(\delta/s)(n-j)-\delta(1+n_{0}/s)-\Delta_{E}

for all
n\geq j\geq 0
, by splitting at
n_{0}
when necessary.
Consequently

(3.10)

\frac{\pi_{n}}{\pi_{j}}\leq Cr^{n-j},\qquad n\geq j\geq 0.

The same bound holds for

\mu_{n}^{(k)}:=\sqrt{\pi_{n}\pi_{n+k}}
, uniformly in
k\geq 0
.
For any of these weights
\mu
, telescoping and the
\ell^{1}
–
\ell^{2}

convolution inequality give

(3.11)

\sum_{n\geq 0}\mu_{n}|u_{n}-u_{0}|^{2}\leq K\sum_{n\geq 1}\mu_{n}|u_{n}-u_{n-1}|^{2},\qquad\sum_{n\geq 0}\mu_{n}\leq K\mu_{0}.

Indeed,

\sqrt{\mu_{n}}(u_{n}-u_{0})=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sqrt{\mu_{n}/\mu_{j}}\sqrt{\mu_{j}}(u_{j}-u_{j-1})
,
and the convolution kernel is bounded by
\sqrt{C}\,r^{(n-j)/2}
.

Decompose finite Fock matrices into diagonals. On the
k
th upper
diagonal write

x=\sum_{n\geq 0}\sqrt{\mu_{n}^{(k)}}\,u_{n}|n\rangle\langle n+k|,\qquad\|x\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{n\geq 0}\mu_{n}^{(k)}|u_{n}|^{2}.

Transforming (
3.6
) by (
2.10
)
and using detailed balance yields

(3.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\geq 1}\mu_{n}^{(k)}\sqrt{d_{n}d_{n+k}}|u_{n}-u_{n-1}|^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n\geq 0}\mu_{n}^{(k)}\bigl[(\sqrt{b_{n}}-\sqrt{b_{n+k}})^{2}+(\sqrt{d_{n}}-\sqrt{d_{n+k}})^{2}\bigr]|u_{n}|^{2}.

To check the identity, expansion gives diagonal coefficient

(b_{n}+b_{n+k}+d_{n}+d_{n+k})/2
; the neighbouring coefficients follow
from
\pi_{n}b_{n}=\pi_{n+1}d_{n+1}
.
For
k=0
, (
3.11
)
gives

\|x\|_{2}^{2}-|\langle\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}},x\rangle|^{2}\leq\sum_{n}\pi_{n}|u_{n}-u_{0}|^{2}\leq\frac{K}{d_{*}}\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}(x).

For
k\geq 1
, the
n=0
term of the second sum in
(
3.12
)
is at least

\mu_{0}^{(k)}d_{*}|u_{0}|^{2}/2
, because
d_{0}=0
. Hence

\displaystyle\|x\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2K\left(\mu_{0}^{(k)}|u_{0}|^{2}+\sum_{n\geq 1}\mu_{n}^{(k)}|u_{n}-u_{n-1}|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4K}{d_{*}}\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}(x).

The lower diagonals follow by adjunction. Distinct diagonals are
orthogonal for the norm and form, so summation and passage through
the form core prove the asserted lower bound.
∎

In particular, the sufficient condition

\sqrt{n}\,\widehat{f}(\pm[h(n+1)-h(n)])\to 0
implies the gap obstruction.
Only the downward rates are needed, and no Schwartz assumption on
the filter is required. For
h(n)=n^{q}
,
q>1
, filters with Gaussian
frequency decay satisfy the condition.

For comparison with the Metropolis–Hastings choice, define

(3.13)

\widehat{f}_{\!\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathscr{M}$}}(\nu):=\exp\left[-\frac{\sqrt{1+\beta^{2}\nu^{2}}+\beta\nu}{4}\right].

Figure 1.
Metropolis-type filter
\widehat{f}_{\!\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathscr{M}$}}
for
\beta=1
.

Corollary 3.3

(Metropolis filter)
.

Assume the standing generation hypotheses for the Metropolis filter
and the Hamiltonian assumptions of Theorem
3.2
.
Then

(3.14)

\operatorname{gap}(L_{\widehat{f}_{\!\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathscr{M}$}},h(N)})\geq\frac{\left(1-e^{-\beta\delta/(2s)}\right)^{2}}{4}\exp\left[-\beta\delta\left(1+\frac{n_{0}}{s}\right)-\frac{\sqrt{1+\beta^{2}\Delta_{E}^{2}}+3\beta\Delta_{E}}{2}\right]>0.

Proof.

Writing
\Delta_{n}=h(n+1)-h(n)
, eventual monotonicity gives

\Delta_{n}\geq-\Delta_{E}
for all
n
. Since
\widehat{f}_{\!\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathscr{M}$}}
is decreasing,

|\widehat{f}_{\!\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathscr{M}$}}(-\Delta_{n})|^{2}\geq|\widehat{f}_{\!\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathscr{M}$}}(\Delta_{E})|^{2}=\exp\left[-\frac{\sqrt{1+\beta^{2}\Delta_{E}^{2}}+\beta\Delta_{E}}{2}\right]=:c_{0}.

Thus
d_{n}\geq c_{0}n
and Theorem
3.2

applies.
∎

3.3.
Polynomially decaying filters

To obtain integrable time-domain filters, we damp the Metropolis
filter at negative frequencies. For
\eta\geq 0
, set

(3.15)

\widehat{f}_{\eta}(\nu):=\frac{\widehat{f}_{\!\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathscr{M}$}}(\nu)}{\langle\beta\nu\rangle^{\eta}}=\frac{\exp[-(\sqrt{1+\beta^{2}\nu^{2}}+\beta\nu)/4]}{(1+\beta^{2}\nu^{2})^{\eta/2}}.

The denominator is even, and thus KMS symmetry is preserved.

Corollary 3.4

(Sharp polynomial decay criterion)
.

Assume the standing generation hypotheses for
\widehat{f}_{\eta}

and the Hamiltonian assumptions of Theorem
3.2
.
Write
\Delta_{n}=h(n+1)-h(n)
and define

(3.16)

M_{\eta}(h):=\sup_{n\geq 0}\frac{\langle\beta\Delta_{n}\rangle^{2\eta}}{n+1}.

Then, for
\eta\geq 0
,

(3.17)

\operatorname{gap}(L_{\widehat{f}_{\eta},h(N)})>0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad M_{\eta}(h)<\infty.

When
M_{\eta}(h)<\infty
, the gap is at least

c_{\beta,h}/M_{\eta}(h)
with
c_{\beta,h}=c_{0}/(4K)
independent of
\eta
.

Proof.

The bounds
c_{0}\leq|\widehat{f}_{\!\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathscr{M}$}}(-\Delta_{n})|^{2}\leq 1
imply

(3.18)

\frac{c_{0}}{M_{\eta}(h)}\leq\inf_{n\geq 1}d_{n}\leq\frac{1}{M_{\eta}(h)},

with
1/\infty=0
. Apply Theorem
3.2
.
∎

For example,

(3.19)

h(n)=n^{q},\quad q>1:\qquad\operatorname{gap}(L_{\widehat{f}_{\eta},H})>0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad 0\leq\eta\leq\frac{1}{2(q-1)}.

For
h(n)=n
, every finite exponent is admissible. The next lemma
provides the time-domain bounds needed for implementation.

Lemma 3.5

(Time-domain integrability)
.

For every
\eta>0
,
\widehat{f}_{\eta}
is the Fourier transform
of a function
f_{\eta}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
. Moreover, for every

0<a<1/\beta
, there is
C_{a,\eta,\beta}<\infty
such that

(3.20)

|f_{\eta}(t)|\leq C_{a,\eta,\beta}\frac{e^{-a|t|}}{|t|},\qquad|t|\geq 1.

Near the origin,

(3.21)

|f_{\eta}(t)|\leq C_{\eta,\beta}\begin{cases}1+|t|^{\eta-1},&0<\eta<1,\\
1+|\log|t||,&\eta=1,\\
1,&\eta>1,\end{cases}\qquad 0<|t|\leq 1.

Proof.

The principal branches in (
3.15
) are
analytic on
|\operatorname{Im}z|<1/\beta
, since

\operatorname{Re}(1+\beta^{2}z^{2})>0
there. Uniformly on each
closed smaller strip,

\widehat{f}_{\eta}(x+iy)=\begin{cases}O(|x|^{-\eta}),&x\to-\infty,\\
O(e^{-\beta x/2}x^{-\eta}),&x\to+\infty,\end{cases}

and its derivative is integrable along every horizontal line
in that strip. For
t\neq 0
, the improper integral

f_{\eta}(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widehat{f}_{\eta}(\nu)e^{-it\nu}\,d\nu

therefore converges by integration by parts. Shifting the
contour
to
\operatorname{Im}z=-a\operatorname{sgn}(t)
and integrating
by parts proves (
3.20
); the vertical
sides vanish by the displayed decay.

At the negative end,

\widehat{f}_{\eta}(-u)=\beta^{-\eta}u^{-\eta}+O(u^{-\eta-1})
as
u\to\infty
. Subtracting this leading
power on
u\geq 1
leaves an
L^{1}
frequency function, whose
inverse transform is bounded. Split the remaining oscillatory
integral at
u=|t|^{-1}
and integrate its tail by parts.
The first piece is bounded by

\int_{1}^{|t|^{-1}}u^{-\eta}\,du
, and the second by a constant
times
|t|^{\eta-1}
. This proves
(
3.21
). Both time-domain bounds
are integrable, so
f_{\eta}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
.

To justify the Fourier identification, for
R\geq 1
set

f_{\eta,R}(t):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-R}^{R}\widehat{f}_{\eta}(\nu)e^{-it\nu}\,d\nu.

The same split at
|t|^{-1}
gives
(
3.21
) for
f_{\eta,R}
with a
constant independent of
R
. Integration by parts also gives

|f_{\eta,R}(t)|\leq\frac{2\|\widehat{f}_{\eta}\|_{\infty}+\|\widehat{f}_{\eta}^{\prime}\|_{1}}{2\pi|t|},\qquad|t|\geq 1.

Since
f_{\eta,R}(t)\to f_{\eta}(t)
for
t\neq 0
, these
bounds allow dominated convergence against every Schwartz test
function. Thus
f_{\eta,R}\to f_{\eta}
in

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
, the space of tempered distributions.
On the frequency side,

\mathbf{1}_{[-R,R]}\widehat{f}_{\eta}\to\widehat{f}_{\eta}
in

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
by dominated convergence as well.
Continuity and invertibility of the Fourier transform on

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
, as stated in

[
39
, Proposition 9.6, p. 60]
,
therefore identify the distributional Fourier transform of

f_{\eta}
with
\widehat{f}_{\eta}
. For an
L^{1}
function, the
distributional transform agrees with its ordinary Fourier
transform by Fubini’s theorem. Both that transform and

\widehat{f}_{\eta}
are continuous, so their distributional equality
implies the pointwise identity

\widehat{f}_{\eta}(\nu)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{\eta}(t)e^{it\nu}\,dt,\qquad\nu\in\mathbb{R}.

∎

4.
Efficient implementation

In Proposition
2.8
, we constructed the generator
\mathcal{L}_{\smash{\widehat{f},H}}
of a quantum dynamical semigroup associated with a function
\widehat{f}
that satisfies minimal summability and symmetry assumptions in
Condition

A
. In Section
3
, we argued that the spectral gap of the corresponding evolution—which governs its mixing time—crucially depends on the choice of
\widehat{f}
. In particular, choosing
\widehat{f}\equiv\widehat{f}_{\!\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathscr{M}$}}(\nu)
as defined in (
3.13
), we found in Corollary
3.3
that the Gibbs sampler corresponding to
H=N^{2}
is gapped; however, this is no longer true for rapidly decaying functions, cf.
Theorem

3.2
. However, the particular choice of (
3.13
) appears to hinder algorithmic implementations of the dynamics, as the lack of decay of
\widehat{f}_{\!\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathscr{M}$}}
as
\nu\to-\infty
implies singularity of its Fourier transform
f_{\!\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathscr{M}$}}
at
0
. Another serious issue compared to the finite-dimensional setting concerns the possible unboundedness of the bare Hamiltonian
H
.

The polynomially decaying filters
\widehat{f}_{\eta}
of
(
3.15
) resolve the first difficulty:
Corollary
3.4
gives their sharp gap
criterion, while Lemma
3.5
shows that,
for every
\eta>0
, their time-domain kernels belong to

L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
and decay exponentially at infinity.

In order to address the
unboundedness of the Hamiltonian, we consider an alternative family of generators for which the efficiency results established in Section
3.1
remain valid, and we show that these generators can be implemented using infinite-dimensional extensions of the LCU technique. Our starting point is the following generator, which is well defined on
\mathscr{F}
for any
\sigma_{E}\in[0,\infty]
, under
Condition

A
, where the Gaussian factor is interpreted as
\mathbf{1}_{\{\nu_{1}=\nu_{2}\}}
at
\sigma_{E}=0
and as
1
at
\sigma_{E}=\infty
:

(4.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}(\rho)\!:=\!\sum_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\sum_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}e^{-\frac{(\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})^{2}}{8\sigma_{E}^{2}}}\,\overline{\widehat{f}(\nu_{1})}\widehat{f}(\nu_{2})A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}\rho(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}+G_{\sigma_{E}}\rho+\rho G_{\sigma_{E}}^{\dagger}\,,

where

\displaystyle G_{\sigma_{E}}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathcal{A}\\
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\end{subarray}}\!e^{-\frac{(\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})^{2}}{8\sigma_{E}^{2}}}\!\!\!\frac{1}{1+e^{\frac{\beta(\nu_{2}-\nu_{1})}{2}}}\overline{\widehat{f}(\nu_{1})}\widehat{f}(\nu_{2})(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}.

Formally, we retrieve the generator
\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},H}
of Proposition
2.8
by setting
\sigma_{E}=\infty
. It is not hard to see, by adapting the generation results of Section
2
, that the above expression gives rise to a KMS-symmetric quantum Markov semigroup for any
\sigma_{E}\geq 0
, whose generator in the Hilbert-Schmidt setting we denote by
L_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
. On the other hand, taking the limit
\sigma_{E}\to 0
, since
B^{\alpha}_{\nu\nu}=0
, we obtain a Davies-type generator
\mathcal{L}_{0,\widehat{f},H}\equiv\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{D}}
, with

(4.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{D}}(\rho)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\\
\nu\in B(H)\end{subarray}}|\widehat{f}(\nu)|^{2}\,\big(A^{\alpha}_{\nu}\rho(A^{\alpha}_{\nu})^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\{(A^{\alpha}_{\nu})^{\dagger}A^{\alpha}_{\nu},\rho\}\big),

that is, the rates
\Upsilon
introduced in (
1.1
) all coincide with
|\widehat{f}|^{2}
. In other words, the family
\{\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}\}_{\sigma_{E}\geq 0}
interpolates between
\mathcal{L}_{\widehat{f},H}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{D}}
. The next sections are organized as follows: first, in Section
4.1
, we briefly justify the well-posedness of the generators
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
and
L_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
, and prove that introducing the Gaussian envelope cannot decrease a positive spectral gap, provided
\ker L_{\widehat{f},H}=\mathbb{C}\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}
. Under this assumption, the positive gap lower bounds of Section
3
derived for
L_{\widehat{f},H}
directly hold for
L_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
for every
0<\sigma_{E}\leq\infty
. In Section
4.2
, we derive an integral formulation of the generator
\mathcal{L}_{\smash{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}}

for
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
. The rest of this Section is devoted to the derivation of an implementation scheme for the evolution generated by
\mathcal{L}_{\smash{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}}
.

4.1.
Gaussian-convoluted generators

We start by considering the following densely defined, symmetric operator defined on
\mathscr{F}_{\sigma_{\beta}}
:

(4.3)

\displaystyle L_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}(\lambda x+\mu\,\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}):=-\lambda\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathcal{A}\\
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in B(H)\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle e^{-\frac{(\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})^{2}}{8\sigma_{E}^{2}}}\,\frac{\overline{\widehat{f}(\nu_{1})}\widehat{f}(\nu_{2})\,e^{\beta(\nu_{1}+\nu_{2})/4}}{2\cosh((\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})\beta/4)}\,(\delta^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}\delta^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}(x)

with associated form

(4.4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}(\lambda\,x+\mu\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}):=|\lambda|^{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathcal{A}\\
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in B(H)\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle e^{-\frac{(\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})^{2}}{8\sigma_{E}^{2}}}\,\frac{\overline{\widehat{f}(\nu_{1})}\widehat{f}(\nu_{2})\,e^{\beta(\nu_{1}+\nu_{2})/4}}{2\cosh((\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})\beta/4)}\,\langle\delta^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}}(x),\delta^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}(x)\rangle.

By Fourier integration, we notice that for all
x\in\mathscr{F}

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}(x)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{E}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\\
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\end{subarray}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\!\!\!e^{-2\sigma_{E}^{2}s^{2}}e^{-is(\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})}\,\frac{\overline{\widehat{f}(\nu_{1})}\widehat{f}(\nu_{2})\,e^{\beta(\nu_{1}+\nu_{2})/4}}{2\cosh((\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})\beta/4)}\,\langle\delta^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}}(x),\delta^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}(x)\rangle\,ds

\displaystyle=\sigma_{E}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\\
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\end{subarray}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\!\!\!e^{-2\sigma_{E}^{2}s^{2}}\frac{\overline{\widehat{f}(\nu_{1})}\widehat{f}(\nu_{2})\,e^{\beta(\nu_{1}+\nu_{2})/4}}{2\cosh((\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})\beta/4)}\,\langle\delta^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}}(x(s)),\delta^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}(x(s))\rangle\,ds

(4.5)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{E}{\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}}\,\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-2\sigma_{E}^{2}s^{2}}\,\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}(x(-s))\,ds

for
x(t):=e^{iHt}xe^{-iHt}
, where we also used that
e^{itH}A^{\alpha}_{\nu}e^{-itH}=e^{it\nu}A^{\alpha}_{\nu}
.
The identity also holds for

x\in\mathscr{F}_{\sigma_{\beta}}
: adding a multiple of

\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}
leaves both forms unchanged, and this Gibbs
square root is fixed by
x\mapsto x(t)
.

This directly shows
that
L_{\smash{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}}
is negative. By Friedrichs extension,
\mathcal{E}_{\smash{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}}
is closable and defines a self-adjoint extension of
L_{\smash{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}}
. Both the close form and operator are denoted as
\mathcal{E}_{\smash{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}}
and
L_{\smash{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}}
, by slight abuse of notations.

Proposition 4.1
.

The closed quadratic form
\mathcal{E}_{\smash{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}}
is completely Dirichlet. Therefore, the strongly continuous semigroup
\{e^{tL_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}}\}_{t\geq 0}
is completely Markov with respect to the state
\sigma_{\beta}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.4
, we know that
\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}
is completely Dirichlet. Therefore, for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathscr{M}_{n}\otimes\mathscr{F}_{\sigma_{\beta}}
, we have that
\mathcal{E}^{(n)}_{\widehat{f},H}(\mathbf{x})\geq\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}^{(n)}(\mathbf{x}_{+}),\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}^{(n)}(\mathbf{x}_{\wedge})
. The result follows since
\mathbf{x}_{+}(s)=(\mathbf{x}(s))_{+}
,
\mathbf{x}_{\wedge}(s)=(\mathbf{x}(s))_{\wedge}
, the integral representation (
4.5
) and the form core property of
\mathscr{F}_{\sigma_{\beta}}
.

∎

Next, we aim at deriving an expression for the generator
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
of the strongly continuous semigroup induced on
\mathscr{T}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
by extension of the reasoning of Lemma
2.6
. In place of the operator
G
defined in (
2.12
), we consider the densely defined operator
G_{\sigma_{E}}
on
\mathcal{F}
as

(4.6)

\displaystyle G_{\sigma_{E}}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathcal{A}\\
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\end{subarray}}\!e^{-\frac{(\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})^{2}}{8\sigma_{E}^{2}}}\!\!\!\frac{1}{1+e^{\frac{\beta(\nu_{2}-\nu_{1})}{2}}}\overline{\widehat{f}(\nu_{1})}\widehat{f}(\nu_{2})(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}})^{\dagger}A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}

and the map
\Phi_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
defined on
\mathscr{F}
as

\displaystyle\Phi_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}(\ket{E_{i}}\bra{E_{j}})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathcal{A}\\
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\end{subarray}}e^{-\frac{(\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})^{2}}{8\sigma_{E}^{2}}}\,\overline{\widehat{f}(\nu_{1})}\widehat{f}(\nu_{2})\,A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{2}}\ket{E_{i}}(A^{\alpha}_{\nu_{1}}\ket{E_{j}})^{\dagger}.

Indeed, that
G_{\sigma_{E}}
, resp.
\Phi_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
, is well-defined on
\mathcal{F}
, resp. on
\mathscr{F}
, follows directly from the estimates in the proof of
Lemma

2.2
in the limit
\sigma_{E}\to\infty
and the fact that
|e^{\smash{-\frac{(\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})^{2}}{8\sigma_{E}^{2}}}}|\leq 1
uniformly over
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}
. Moreover, by direct computation we have that, as argued in the proof of
Proposition

2.8
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}(\ket{E_{i}}\bra{E_{j}})=G_{\sigma_{E}}\ket{E_{i}}\bra{E_{j}}+\ket{E_{i}}(G_{\sigma_{E}}\ket{E_{j}})^{\dagger}+\Phi_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}(\ket{E_{i}}\bra{E_{j}}).

Next, we show that a positive spectral gap cannot decrease as

\sigma_{E}
decreases, provided the larger-width generator has a simple
kernel (see also
[
8
]
for a finite-dimensional
analogue of the result):

Proposition 4.2
.

For
0<\sigma_{E}\leq\sigma^{\prime}_{E}\leq\infty
, assume that
\ker L_{\sigma^{\prime}_{E},\widehat{f},H}=\mathbb{C}\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}
and
\operatorname{gap}(L_{\sigma^{\prime}_{E},\widehat{f},H})>0
.
Then
\ker L_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}=\mathbb{C}\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}
and

\operatorname{gap}(L_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H})\geq\operatorname{gap}(L_{\sigma^{\prime}_{E},\widehat{f},H}).

Moreover,
\sigma_{\beta}
is the unique invariant density operator
of the semigroup generated by
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
, i.e.
\ker\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}=\mathbb{C}\sigma_{\beta}.

Proof.

We make use of the variational formulation of the gap:

\displaystyle\operatorname{gap}(L_{\sigma^{\prime}_{E},\widehat{f},H})=\inf_{x\in D(\mathcal{E}_{\sigma^{\prime}_{E},\widehat{f},H})\backslash\mathbb{C}\sigma_{\beta}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{\mathcal{E}_{\sigma^{\prime}_{E},\widehat{f},H}(x)}{\Big\|x-\langle\sigma_{\beta}^{\frac{1}{2}},x\rangle\,\sigma_{\beta}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big\|_{2}^{2}},

where
\mathcal{E}_{\smash{\sigma^{\prime}_{E},\widehat{f},H}}
is the Dirichlet form associated with
L_{\smash{\sigma^{\prime}_{E},\widehat{f},H}}
and we used the assumption
\ker L_{\sigma^{\prime}_{E},\widehat{f},H}=\mathbb{C}\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}
.
The case
\sigma_{E}=\sigma^{\prime}_{E}
is immediate, so assume that
\sigma_{E}<\sigma^{\prime}_{E}
.
Therefore, denoting the probability density
g_{\sigma_{E}}(s):=\sigma_{E}\sqrt{2/\pi}e^{-2\sigma_{E}^{2}s^{2}}
and using (
4.5
) as well as the form core property of
\mathscr{F}_{\sigma_{\beta}}
, for all
x\in D(\mathcal{E}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H})\backslash\mathbb{C}\sigma_{\beta}^{\smash{\frac{1}{2}}}
, there exists a sequence of elements
x_{n}\in\mathscr{F}_{\sigma_{\beta}}\backslash\mathbb{C}\sigma_{\beta}^{\smash{\frac{1}{2}}}
such that
\|x-x_{n}\|_{D(\mathcal{E}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H})}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
and

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{E}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}(x)}{\Big\|x-\langle\sigma_{\beta}^{\frac{1}{2}},x\rangle\,\sigma_{\beta}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big\|_{2}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathcal{E}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}(x_{n})}{\Big\|x_{n}-\langle\sigma_{\beta}^{\frac{1}{2}},x_{n}\rangle\,\sigma_{\beta}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big\|_{2}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{g_{\sigma_{E}}(s)\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}(x_{n}(-s))}{\Big\|x_{n}(-s)-\langle\sigma_{\beta}^{\frac{1}{2}},x_{n}(-s)\rangle\,\sigma_{\beta}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big\|_{2}^{2}}\,ds,

where we also used the invariance of the denominator under the unitaries
e^{isH}
.
Now, for any such
x
, denoting
\mathcal{E}_{n}(s):=\mathcal{E}_{\smash{\widehat{f},H}}(x_{n}(s))
, the above equations show that
\mathcal{E}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}(x_{n})=\mathcal{E}_{n}\ast g_{\sigma_{E}}(0)
.
For
\sigma^{\prime}_{E}<\infty
, since
g_{\sigma_{E}}=g_{\sigma^{\prime}_{E}}\ast g_{\sigma_{E}^{*}}
with
(\sigma_{E}^{*})^{2}:=\sigma_{E}^{2}(\sigma^{\prime}_{E})^{2}/((\sigma^{\prime}_{E})^{2}-\sigma_{E}^{2})
, and by associativity of the convolution, we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}(x_{n})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}_{n}\ast g_{\sigma_{E}}(0)=\mathcal{E}_{n}\ast g_{\sigma^{\prime}_{E}}\ast g_{\sigma_{E}^{*}}(0)

\displaystyle=(s\mapsto\mathcal{E}_{\sigma^{\prime}_{E},\widehat{f},H}(x_{n}(s)))\ast g_{\sigma_{E}^{*}}(0).

For
\sigma^{\prime}_{E}=\infty
, the same identity holds with
\sigma_{E}^{*}:=\sigma_{E}
.
Thus, using the variational formulation above and that
g_{\sigma_{E}^{*}}
is a probability density, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{E}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}(x_{n})}{\Big\|x_{n}-\langle\sigma_{\beta}^{\frac{1}{2}},x_{n}\rangle\,\sigma_{\beta}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big\|_{2}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{g_{\sigma_{E}^{*}}(s)\mathcal{E}_{\sigma^{\prime}_{E},\widehat{f},H}(x_{n}(-s))}{\Big\|x_{n}(-s)-\langle\sigma_{\beta}^{\frac{1}{2}},x_{n}(-s)\rangle\,\sigma_{\beta}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big\|_{2}^{2}}\,ds

\displaystyle\geq\operatorname{gap}(L_{\sigma^{\prime}_{E},\widehat{f},H}).

Passing to the limit
n\to\infty
, the same lower bound holds for the quotient at
x
.
Since
\operatorname{gap}(L_{\sigma^{\prime}_{E},\widehat{f},H})>0
, this gives
\ker L_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}=\mathbb{C}\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}
.
We can therefore apply the variational formulation to
L_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
, from which we directly read off that
\operatorname{gap}(L_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H})\geq\operatorname{gap}(L_{\sigma^{\prime}_{E},\widehat{f},H})
.
Finally, convergence to the stationary projection established in Section
2
gives
\ker\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}=\mathbb{C}\sigma_{\beta}
, and hence uniqueness of the invariant density operator.
∎

Corollary 4.3

(Downward rates under Gaussian convolution)
.

Under the hypotheses of Theorem
3.2
and with
d_{*}
defined in (
3.8
),
for every
0<\sigma_{E}\leq\infty
,

\operatorname{gap}(L_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},h(N)})>0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad d_{*}>0.

When
d_{*}>0
, the gap is at least
d_{*}/(4K)
, independently of
\sigma_{E}
.
In particular, the polynomial filters
\widehat{f}_{\eta}
defined in
(
3.15
),
\eta\geq 0
, satisfy

(4.7)

\operatorname{gap}(L_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f}_{\eta},h(N)})>0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad M_{\eta}(h)<\infty,

where
M_{\eta}(h)
is defined in (
3.16
),
with lower bound
c_{\beta,h}/M_{\eta}(h)
whenever
M_{\eta}(h)<\infty
.

Proof.

Proposition
4.2

transfers the lower bound of
Theorem
3.2

to every Gaussian width.
If
d_{*}=0
, strict positivity of the individual rates supplies a
subsequence tending to zero. The necessity proof of Theorem
3.2

applies unchanged for every
0<\sigma_{E}\leq\infty
: its diagonal test
vectors
x_{n}
commute with
H
, so (
4.5
) gives

\mathcal{E}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}(x_{n})=\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{f},H}(x_{n})=\pi_{n}d_{n}
for finite
\sigma_{E}
.
Corollary
2.12
gives

\ker L_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},h(N)}=\mathbb{C}\sqrt{\sigma_{\beta}}

at every such width. The same variational bound therefore yields zero
spectral gap at every width.
The result for the polynomial filter follows from

(
3.18
)
.
∎

4.2.
Integral representation

Next, we provide an integral formulation of the generator
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
for any
\sigma_{E}\in(0,\infty)
and sufficiently nice functions
\widehat{f}
. This form will play a crucial role when considering an implementation of the associated evolution on a discrete variable quantum platform. We assume
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
,
with the Fourier-transform convention stated after
(
1.2
).
All operator integrals below are understood strongly: after
application to a vector in the stated domain, they are Bochner
integrals in
\mathcal{H}
; the completely positive part is
integrated in trace norm. No smoothness of
f
is required.

We start by providing integral representations for each constituent of the generator
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
.

Proposition 4.4
.

Assume Condition
A
is satisfied for some
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
with Fourier transform
\widehat{f}(\nu):=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(s)e^{is\nu}ds
. Then for any
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
,
(L^{\alpha},\mathcal{F})
extends to an operator in
D(\widetilde{H}^{\gamma}),
which is relatively
\widetilde{H}^{\gamma}
-bounded, and for any
\ket{\psi}\in D(\widetilde{H}^{\gamma})
,

\displaystyle L^{\alpha}\ket{\psi}=\int f(s)\,e^{isH}A^{\alpha}e^{-isH}\ket{\psi}\,ds=\sum_{E,E^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}\widehat{f}(E^{\prime}-E)P_{E^{\prime}}A^{\alpha}P_{E}\ket{\psi}.

where the latter expression is to be understood as a limit of the finite sums
\sum_{\smash{E\leq M,E^{\prime}\leq M^{\prime}}}
in
\mathcal{H}
. Moreover,
\widetilde{H}^{\gamma}L^{\alpha}\widetilde{H}^{-\mu}
extends to a bounded operator with integral representation

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}^{\gamma}L^{\alpha}\widetilde{H}^{-\mu}:=\int f(s)e^{isH}\widetilde{H}^{\gamma}A^{\alpha}\widetilde{H}^{-\mu}e^{-isH}\,ds.

Next,
(G_{\sigma_{E}},\mathcal{F})
extends to an operator on
D(\widetilde{H}^{\mu}),
which is relatively
\widetilde{H}^{\mu}
-bounded, and for any
\sigma_{E}\in(0,\infty)
,
\ket{\psi}\in D(\widetilde{H}^{\mu})
,
\ket{\psi}\in D((L^{\alpha})^{\dagger}L^{\alpha})\cap D(G_{\sigma_{E}})
and

\displaystyle G_{\sigma_{E}}\ket{\psi}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}g(t)\,e^{itH}(L^{\alpha})^{\dagger}L^{\alpha}e^{-itH}\ket{\psi}\,dt

\displaystyle=-\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathcal{A}\\
E,E^{\prime},E^{\prime\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\widehat{g}(E^{\prime}-E^{\prime\prime})\overline{\widehat{f}(E-E^{\prime})}\widehat{f}(E-E^{\prime\prime})P_{E^{\prime}}(A^{\alpha})^{\dagger}P_{E}A^{\alpha}P_{E^{\prime\prime}}\ket{\psi},

with
g(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-\nu^{2}/8\sigma_{E}^{2}}}{1+e^{\beta\nu/2}}e^{-i\nu t}d\nu
. Similarly,
\Phi_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
extends to an operator on
D(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\gamma,\gamma})
, which is relatively
\mathcal{W}^{\gamma,\gamma}_{H}
bounded, and for any
x\in D(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\gamma,\gamma})

(4.8)

\displaystyle\Phi_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}(x)=\sigma_{E}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sum_{\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-2\sigma_{E}^{2}s^{2}}\,\,X^{\alpha}_{s}\cdot x\cdot(X_{s}^{\alpha})^{\dagger}\,ds

with
X^{\alpha}_{s}:=e^{isH}L^{\alpha}e^{-isH}
and where we recall that
X_{s}^{\alpha}\cdot x\cdot(X_{s}^{\alpha})^{\dagger}\equiv X_{s}^{\alpha}\widetilde{H}^{-\gamma}(X^{\alpha}_{s}\widetilde{H}^{-\gamma}\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\gamma,\gamma}(x))^{\dagger})^{\dagger}
.

Proof.

Strong continuity of the unitary group,
first on the weighted domains and then after applying the bounded
weighted jump operators, ensures measurability. Absolute
integrability follows from

\displaystyle\|L^{\alpha}\widetilde{H}^{-\gamma}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|f\|_{1}\|A^{\alpha}\widetilde{H}^{-\gamma}\|,

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{H}^{\gamma}L^{\alpha}\widetilde{H}^{-\mu}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|f\|_{1}\|\widetilde{H}^{\gamma}A^{\alpha}\widetilde{H}^{-\mu}\|.

The analogous first bound holds for the adjoints, using

\overline{f}
and
(A^{\alpha})^{\dagger}
. These estimates justify
the dominated-convergence arguments below under the sole filter
assumption
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
.

For
\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{F}
the operator
L^{\alpha}
defined in (
2.11
) satisfies

\displaystyle L^{\alpha}\ket{\psi}

\displaystyle=\sum_{E,E^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}\widehat{f}(E^{\prime}-E)P_{E^{\prime}}A^{\alpha}P_{E}\ket{\psi}=\sum_{E,E^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(s)e^{is(E^{\prime}-E)}dsP_{E^{\prime}}A^{\alpha}P_{E}\ket{\psi}

\displaystyle=\lim_{M\to\infty}\lim_{M^{\prime}\to\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(s)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}E,E^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)\\
E\leq M,\,E^{\prime}\leq M^{\prime}\end{subarray}}e^{is(E^{\prime}-E)}dsP_{E^{\prime}}A^{\alpha}P_{E}\ket{\psi}

\displaystyle=\lim_{M\to\infty}\lim_{M^{\prime}\to\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(s)\,P_{H\leq M^{\prime}}e^{isH}A^{\alpha}e^{-isH}P_{H\leq M}\ket{\psi}\,ds

where we denoted for
M\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
the projection
P_{H\leq M}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}E\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)\\
E\leq M\end{subarray}}P_{E}.
Note that

\displaystyle\|P_{H\leq M^{\prime}}e^{isH}A^{\alpha}e^{-isH}P_{H\leq M}\ket{\psi}\|\leq\|A^{\alpha}\widetilde{H}^{-\gamma}\|\|\widetilde{H}^{\gamma}\ket{\psi}\|

for all
M,M^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
and therefore, using
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}),
we can use dominated convergence to exchange the limit
M,M^{\prime}\to\infty
with the integral and obtain from the above

\displaystyle L^{\alpha}\ket{\psi}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\,f(s)\sum_{E,E^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}e^{is(E^{\prime}-E)}P_{E^{\prime}}A^{\alpha}P_{E}\ket{\psi}\,ds=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(s)e^{isH}A^{\alpha}e^{-isH}\ket{\psi}\,ds\,.

Clearly, the above swapping of sums and integral extend to
\ket{\psi}\in D(\widetilde{H}^{\gamma})
, . We argue similarly for
G_{\sigma_{E}}
as defined in (
4.6
): for any
\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{F}
,

\displaystyle G_{\sigma_{E}}\ket{\psi}

\displaystyle\,=-\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\lim_{M,M^{\prime}\to\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}E,E^{\prime},E^{\prime\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)\\
E\leq M,E^{\prime}\leq M^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\!\!\!g(t)\overline{\widehat{f}(E-E^{\prime})}\widehat{f}(E-E\prime\prime)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\times e^{itH}P_{E^{\prime}}(A^{\alpha})^{\dagger}P_{E}A^{\alpha}e^{-itH}P_{E^{\prime\prime}}\ket{\psi}dt

\displaystyle\,=\!-\!\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\lim_{M,M^{\prime}\to\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\!\!P_{H\leq M^{\prime}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{E,E^{\prime},E^{\prime\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!g(t)\overline{\widehat{f}(E-E^{\prime})}\widehat{f}(E-E^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\times e^{itH}P_{E^{\prime}}(A^{\alpha})^{\dagger}P_{E}P_{H\leq M}A^{\alpha}e^{-itH}P_{E^{\prime\prime}}\ket{\psi}dt

\displaystyle\,=\!-\!\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\lim_{M,M^{\prime}\to\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\!\,g(t)e^{itH}P_{H\leq M^{\prime}}(L^{\alpha})^{\dagger}P_{H\leq M}L^{\alpha}e^{-itH}\ket{\psi}dt.

Moreover, the term above is uniformly integrable, since
g\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
and

\displaystyle\|P_{H\leq M^{\prime}}(L^{\alpha})^{\dagger}P_{H\leq M}L^{\alpha}e^{-itH}\ket{\psi}\|\leq\|\widetilde{H}^{-\gamma}L^{\alpha}\|\,\|\widetilde{H}^{\gamma}L^{\alpha}\widetilde{H}^{-\mu}\|\,\|\widetilde{H}^{\mu}\ket{\psi}\|<\infty

Therefore, using the dominated convergence theorem to exchange the limits
M,M^{\prime}\to\infty
with the integral gives

\displaystyle G_{\sigma_{E}}\ket{\psi}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\alpha}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}g(t)\,\sum_{E,E^{\prime},E^{\prime\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}\overline{\widehat{f}(E-E^{\prime})}\widehat{f}(E-E\prime\prime)e^{itH}P_{E^{\prime}}(A^{\alpha})^{\dagger}P_{E}A^{\alpha}e^{-itH}P_{E\prime\prime}\ket{\psi}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\alpha}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}g(t)\,e^{itH}(L^{\alpha})^{\dagger}L^{\alpha}e^{-itH}\ket{\psi}

where in the last line we used that
L^{\alpha}\ket{\psi}\in D(\widetilde{H}^{\gamma})
.
Once again, the above swapping of sums and integrals extend to
\ket{\psi}\in D(\widetilde{H}^{\mu})
.

For the CP-term
\Phi_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
we apply a similar dominated convergence argument as for the
G_{\sigma_{E}}
operator to exchange the Bohr frequency series with the integral: given
x\in\mathscr{F}

\displaystyle\Phi_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}(x)

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathcal{A}\\
E_{1},E_{1}^{\prime},E_{2},E_{2}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)\end{subarray}}e^{-\frac{(E_{1}-E_{1}^{\prime}-E_{2}+E_{2}^{\prime})^{2}}{8\sigma_{E}^{2}}}\,\overline{\widehat{f}(E_{1}-E_{1}^{\prime})}\widehat{f}(E_{2}-E_{2}^{\prime})\,P_{E_{2}}A^{\alpha}P_{E_{2}^{\prime}}xP_{E_{1}^{\prime}}(A^{\alpha})^{\dagger}P_{E_{1}}

\displaystyle\quad=\sigma_{E}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathcal{A}\\
E_{1},E_{1}^{\prime},E_{2},E_{2}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-2\sigma_{E}^{2}s^{2}}e^{is(-E_{1}+E_{1}^{\prime}+E_{2}-E_{2}^{\prime})}\overline{\widehat{f}(E_{1}-E_{1}^{\prime})}\widehat{f}(E_{2}-E_{2}^{\prime})\,P_{E_{2}}A^{\alpha}P_{E_{2}^{\prime}}xP_{E_{1}^{\prime}}(A^{\alpha})^{\dagger}P_{E_{1}}ds

\displaystyle\quad=\sigma_{E}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathcal{A}\\
E_{1},E_{1}^{\prime},E_{2},E_{2}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\!\!\!\!\!e^{-2\sigma_{E}^{2}s^{2}}\overline{\widehat{f}(E_{1}-E_{1}^{\prime})}\widehat{f}(E_{2}-E_{2}^{\prime})\,P_{E_{2}}e^{isH}A^{\alpha}e^{-isH}P_{E_{2}^{\prime}}xe^{isH}P_{E_{1}^{\prime}}(A^{\alpha})^{\dagger}e^{-isH}P_{E_{1}}ds.

Arguing by truncation as above, it suffices to show that

\displaystyle P_{H\leq M}\sum_{E_{1},E_{2},E_{1}^{\prime},E_{2}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}\overline{\widehat{f}(E_{1}-E_{1}^{\prime})}\widehat{f}(E_{2}-E_{2}^{\prime})\,P_{E_{2}}e^{isH}A^{\alpha}e^{-isH}P_{E_{2}^{\prime}}xe^{isH}P_{E_{1}^{\prime}}(A^{\alpha})^{\dagger}e^{-isH}P_{E_{1}}P_{H\leq M^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\qquad=P_{H\leq M}\,\sum_{E_{2}^{\prime},E_{1}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(H)}\,e^{isH}L^{\alpha}e^{-isH}P_{E_{2}^{\prime}}xP_{E_{1}^{\prime}}e^{isH}(L^{\alpha})^{\dagger}e^{-isH}P_{H\leq M^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\qquad=P_{H\leq M}\,\,e^{isH}L^{\alpha}e^{-isH}xe^{isH}(L^{\alpha})^{\dagger}e^{-isH}P_{H\leq M^{\prime}}

with norm

\displaystyle\|P_{H\leq M}\,\,e^{isH}L^{\alpha}e^{-isH}xe^{isH}(L^{\alpha})^{\dagger}e^{-isH}P_{H\leq M^{\prime}}\|_{1}\leq\|x\|_{\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\gamma,\gamma}}\|L^{\alpha}\widetilde{H}^{-\gamma}\|^{2}<\infty.

Thus, once again, by the dominated convergence theorem, we conclude that

\displaystyle\Phi_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}(x)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{E}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-2\sigma_{E}^{2}s^{2}}\,e^{isH}L^{\alpha}e^{-isH}\cdot x\cdot e^{isH}(L^{\alpha})^{\dagger}e^{-isH}ds

\displaystyle=\sigma_{E}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sum_{\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-2\sigma_{E}^{2}s^{2}}\,\,X_{s}^{\alpha}\cdot x\cdot(X_{s}^{\alpha})^{\dagger}\,ds.

Again, the same swap holds and extends to
x\in\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\gamma,\gamma}
.

∎

Corollary 4.5
.

Assume that
Condition

A
is satisfied for some
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
with Fourier transform
\widehat{f}(\nu):=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(s)e^{is\nu}ds
. Then,
D(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\gamma,\gamma})\cap D(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{0,\mu})\cap D(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\mu,0})\subseteq D(\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H})
, and for any
x\in D(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\gamma,\gamma})\cap D(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{0,\mu})\cap D(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\mu,0})
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}(x):=\Phi_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}(x)+G_{\sigma_{E}}\cdot x+x\cdot G_{\sigma_{E}}^{\dagger}.

Proof.

This follows the same lines as in
Proposition

2.10
, by invoking the closedness of
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
and density of
\mathscr{F}
in
D(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\gamma,\gamma})\cap D(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{0,\mu})\cap D(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\mu,0})
:
given
x\in D(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\gamma,\gamma})\cap D(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{0,\mu})\cap D(\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\mu,0})
,
x_{n}:=P_{H\leq n}xP_{H\leq n}\in\mathcal{W}_{H}^{\mu,0}
with
x_{n}\to x
in
\mathscr{T}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
as
n\to\infty
.
Then, by
Proposition

4.4
,

\displaystyle G_{\sigma_{E}}\cdot x_{n}=-\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}g(t)e^{itH}(L^{\alpha})^{\dagger}L^{\alpha}e^{-itH}x_{n}dt\to-\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}g(t)e^{itH}(L^{\alpha})^{\dagger}L^{\alpha}e^{-itH}xdt\equiv G_{\sigma_{E}}\cdot x

by dominated convergence theorem. Similarly, we show that
x_{n}\cdot G_{\sigma_{E}}^{\dagger}\to x\cdot G_{\sigma_{E}}^{\dagger}
and

\displaystyle\Phi_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}(x_{n})=\sigma_{E}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-2\sigma_{E}^{2}s^{2}}X_{s}^{\alpha}\cdot x_{n}\cdot(X_{s}^{\alpha})^{\dagger}\,ds\to\sigma_{E}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-2\sigma_{E}^{2}s^{2}}X_{s}^{\alpha}\cdot x\cdot(X_{s}^{\alpha})^{\dagger}\,ds.

The result follows.
∎

4.3.
Finite-dimensional truncations

In this section we consider a finite-dimensional
4
4

4

On the original Hilbert space the truncated generator is bounded, generally not a finite-rank superoperator. Its restriction to the reducing system register is finite dimensional.
truncation of the unbounded generator
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
which for truncation parameter
M\in\mathbb{N}
is denoted by
\mathcal{L}^{\leq M}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H_{\leq M}}.

We show in the following that
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
is well-approximated by the generator
\mathcal{L}^{\leq M}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H_{\leq M}}

on certain energy constrained input states and for truncation parameter
M
large enough.

In Section
4.3.1
, we first introduce the bounded generator
\mathcal{L}^{\leq M}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
by truncating the bare jumps
A^{\alpha}
within the unbounded generator and continue to show closeness of this bounded generator to
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}.
Then in Section
4.3.2
we further truncate the Hamiltonian to define the finite-dimensional generator
\mathcal{L}^{\leq M}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H_{\leq M}}
and show closeness to
\mathcal{L}^{\leq M}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}.
Lastly, we show in Section
4.3.3
that the dynamics
e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}}
is well approximated by the finite-dimensional one
e^{t\mathcal{L}^{\leq M}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H_{\leq M}}}.

4.3.1.
Truncating the bare jumps

In the following section we focus on approximating bare jumps
\{A^{\alpha}\}
by finite-dimensional
5
5

5

Technically, the single-mode truncated bare jumps are finite rank on the original infinite-dimensional space. Their tensor extensions to several modes are bounded local operators and generally have infinite rank . A finite-dimensional system register is introduced in Section
4.3.2
.
bare jumps which will be useful for implementing the Lindlabian dynamics considered in the previous sections on finite-dimensional hardware.

To illustrate this, let us focus first on bare jumps being the annihilation and creation operators. In particular for truncation parameter
M\in\mathbb{N},
we consider
\pi_{M}:=\sum_{n=0}^{M}|n\rangle\!\langle n|
and

(4.9)

\displaystyle a^{\leq M}:=\pi_{M}a\pi_{M}=\sum_{n=1}^{M}\sqrt{n}\ket{n-1}\!\bra{n},\quad\text{and}\quad\left(a^{\leq M}\right)^{\dagger}=\pi_{M}a^{\dagger}\pi_{M}=\sum_{n=0}^{M-1}\sqrt{n+1}\ket{n+1}\!\bra{n}.

More generally, for
k\in\mathbb{N},
we can also consider truncations of higher order bare jumps like
a^{k}
and
(a^{k})^{\dagger}
which are defined by

\displaystyle\left(a^{k}\right)^{\leq M}

\displaystyle:=\pi_{M}a^{k}\pi_{M}=\sum_{n=k}^{M}\sqrt{\frac{n!}{(n-k)!}}\ket{n-k}\!\bra{n},\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\left((a^{k})^{\leq M}\right)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\pi_{M}(a^{\dagger})^{k}\pi_{M}=\sum_{n=0}^{M-k}\sqrt{\frac{(n+k)!}{n!}}\ket{n+k}\!\bra{n}.

In the following lemma we see that these finite-dimensional truncations approximate the untruncated bare jumps well when applied on energy constrained input states.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
\kappa\in(0,1],

k\in\mathbb{N}
and
M\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
M\geq\left(\frac{k}{2\kappa}\right)^{1/\kappa}+k.
Then for
\ket{\psi}\in D(e^{N^{\kappa}})
we have

\displaystyle\left\|\left(a^{k}-(a^{k})^{\leq M}\right)\ket{\psi}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq M^{k/2}\,e^{-M^{\kappa}}\|e^{N^{\kappa}}\ket{\psi}\|,

\displaystyle\left\|\left((a^{k})^{\dagger}-\left((a^{k})^{\leq M}\right)^{\dagger}\right)\ket{\psi}\right\|

\displaystyle\lesssim M^{k/2}\,e^{-M^{\kappa}}\|e^{N^{\kappa}}\ket{\psi}\|,

Furthermore, for
\psi\in D(e^{2N^{\kappa}})
we have

\displaystyle\left\|e^{N^{\kappa}}(a^{k}-(a^{k})^{\leq M})\ket{\psi}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq M^{k/2}\,e^{-M^{\kappa}}\|e^{2N^{\kappa}}\ket{\psi}\|,

\displaystyle\left\|e^{N^{\kappa}}\left((a^{k})^{\dagger}-\left((a^{k})^{\leq M}\right)^{\dagger}\right)\ket{\psi}\right\|

\displaystyle\lesssim M^{k/2}\,e^{-M^{\kappa}}\|e^{2N^{\kappa}}\ket{\psi}\|.

The constants hidden in the
\lesssim
-notation in the above inequalities depend on
k
and
\kappa
but on no other variables.

Proof.

The
first inequality follows from noting

\displaystyle\left\|(a^{k}-(a^{k})^{\leq M})e^{-N^{\kappa}}\right\|=\sup_{n\geq\max\{M+1,k\}}\sqrt{\frac{n!}{(n-k)!}}e^{-n^{\kappa}}\leq\sup_{n\geq\max\{M+1,k\}}n^{k/2}e^{-n^{\kappa}}\leq M^{k/2}e^{-M^{\kappa}},

where for the last inequality we have used that
M\geq\left(\frac{k}{2\kappa}\right)^{1/\kappa}+k
and that the function
x^{k/2}e^{-x^{\kappa}}
is non-increasing for
x\geq\left(\frac{k}{2\kappa}\right)^{1/\kappa}.

Similarly, we see

\displaystyle\left\|\left((a^{k})^{\dagger}-\left((a^{k})^{\leq M}\right)^{\dagger}\right)e^{-N^{\kappa}}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{n\geq M-k+1}\sqrt{\frac{(n+k)!}{n!}}e^{-n^{\kappa}}\leq\sup_{n\geq M-k+1}(n+k)^{k/2}e^{-n^{\kappa}}

\displaystyle\leq e^{\kappa(k-1)}(M+1)^{k/2}e^{-M^{\kappa}},

where for the last inequality we have used that the function
(x+k)^{k/2}e^{-x^{\kappa}}
is non-increasing for
x\geq\left(\frac{k}{2\kappa}\right)^{1/\kappa}
and furthermore that
e^{M^{\kappa}-(M-k+1)^{\kappa}}\leq e^{\kappa(k-1)}.

The third and fourth inequality follow similarly while noting for the fourth inequality that
e^{(n+k)^{\kappa}-n^{\kappa}}\leq e^{k\kappa}
for
n\geq 1
, which is the range of the tail indices in that estimate.
∎

Going beyond, we want to consider a finite-dimensional truncation scheme for a set of general bare jumps
\{A^{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}
on some separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}:

For
M\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
we consider projections
\pi^{\alpha}_{M}
of rank
M+1
on the acted-on factor; in a multimode system they may be tensored with identities on the other factors. Assuming that
\pi^{\alpha}_{M}\mathcal{H}\subseteq D(A^{\alpha})
we then define the truncated bare jump
\left(A^{\alpha}\right)^{\leq M}:=\pi^{\alpha}_{M}A^{\alpha}\pi^{\alpha}_{M}.
We assume that we have good control on the operator norm of the truncated jump, i.e. precisely

(4.10)

\displaystyle\left\|(A^{\alpha})^{\leq M}\right\|\leq q^{\prime}(M),

for some polynomially bounded function
q^{\prime}(M).

We choose
(A^{\bar{\alpha}})^{\leq M}=((A^{\alpha})^{\leq M})^{\dagger}
whenever
A^{\bar{\alpha}}=(A^{\alpha})^{\dagger}
.

For the remaining section we assume that
A^{\alpha}
is well-approximated by the truncated bare jump on energy constrained inputs: Precisely, to measure energy we consider a self-adjoint and positive semidefinite operator,
N_{\mathcal{A}},
and assume that for
l=1,2
and some
\kappa\in(0,1/2)
we have

(4.11)

\displaystyle\left\|e^{(l-1)N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}(A^{\alpha}-(A^{\alpha})^{\leq M})e^{-lN_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}\right\|\leq q(M)e^{-M^{\kappa}},

where
q(M)
is some polynomially bounded function. For certain parts of the proofs of Section
4.3.2
we also assume that

(4.12)

\displaystyle\left\|e^{N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}(A^{\alpha})^{\leq M}e^{-N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}\right\|\leq q^{\prime}(M)\quad\text{and}\quad\left\|e^{-N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}(A^{\alpha})^{\leq M}e^{N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}\right\|\leq q^{\prime}(M)

for the polynomially bounded function
q^{\prime}(M)
which also appeared in (
4.10
).

To illustrate the above truncation scheme, we consider the example of an
m
-mode bosonic system on the Hilbert space
\left(L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\right)^{\otimes m}
: In this case, we can consider multi-indices
\alpha
taken from the set
\mathcal{A}=\{(i,+),(i,-)\}_{i=1}^{m}
and bare jumps
\{A^{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}=\left\{a^{k}_{i},(a_{i}^{k})^{\dagger}\right\}_{i=1}^{m}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}.
Furthermore, for
\alpha=(i,+)
or
\alpha=(i,-)
, we consider the specific choice of projections with local rank
M+1
, given by local truncations in the Fock basis, i.e.,
\pi^{\alpha}_{M}\equiv\pi^{i}_{M}=\mathbbm{1}_{1,\cdots,i-1}\otimes\pi_{M}\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{i+1,\cdots,m}
, where
\pi_{M}=\sum_{n=0}^{M}|n\rangle\!\langle n|.

In this case, (
4.10
) holds with
M^{k/2}
; to satisfy (
4.12
) with the same polynomial, take
q^{\prime}(M)=C_{k,\kappa}(M+1)^{k/2}
.
Lemma
4.6
ensures for this particular choice of bare jumps and truncations that the relation (
4.11
) holds, where in this case we take
N_{\mathcal{A}}=N_{\operatorname{tot}}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}N_{i}.
Alternatively, in
[
40
]
we consider
N_{\mathcal{A}}
to be a finite rank perturbation of
N_{\operatorname{tot}}
and verify relation (
4.11
) for this choice as well.

Next, we consider the generator
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
with bare jumps
\{A^{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}
, for which Proposition
4.4
provided an explicit integral representation. We see in the following that
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
is close to the generator
\mathcal{L}^{\leq M}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
which is defined by replacing
A^{\alpha}
by
\left(A^{\alpha}\right)^{\leq M}.
For this we assume that energy measured with respect
e^{2N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}
can only increase subexponentially with respect to time when evolved with the unitary dynamics generated by
H
: More precisely we assume for
k=2,4
and
\kappa\in(0,1/2)
as above that there exists
r\geq 0
such that for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
we have
6
6

6

The reason for the choice of range of
\kappa,
in particular the constraint
\kappa<1/2
, is that this enables us to prove approximation of
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
by
\mathcal{L}^{\leq M}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
for all
\beta>0.
The reason for this is that the function
e^{r|t|^{2\kappa}/2}
needs to be dominated by the function
g(t),
which is featured in the integral representations of the generators stated in Proposition
4.4
, and it can be shown that
|g(t)|\lesssim e^{-\tfrac{\pi}{\beta}|t|}
for large
t.

Throughout the truncation and circuit results, the condition

0<\kappa<1/2
,
r\geq 0
may alternatively be replaced by

0<\kappa\leq 1
,
r=0
, with all other hypotheses retained.
Indeed, every time-growth factor
e^{r|t|^{2\kappa}}
is then one,
so neither subadditivity of
|t|^{2\kappa}
nor its domination by the
exponentially decaying time kernel is needed. The ladder estimates
use only concavity of
x^{\kappa}
and remain valid up to
\kappa=1
.
In particular, when
[H,N_{\mathcal{A}}]=0
and the required Gibbs moment is finite,

\kappa=1
gives an error polynomial in
M
times
e^{-M}
and a cutoff
of logarithmic, rather than a higher logarithmic-power, order.

(4.13)

\displaystyle e^{-itH}e^{kN_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}e^{itH}\leq e^{r|t|^{2\kappa}}\,e^{kN_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}.

Such a condition is natural and holds, e.g., for the choice
N_{\mathcal{A}}=N_{\operatorname{tot}}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}N_{i}
and the Bose-Hubbard model and for all
\kappa\in(0,1]
and
r=0
since the corresponding Hamiltonian commutes with
N_{\operatorname{tot}}
, and, as we see in Lemma
4.9
below, for the mean field Bose-Hubbard Hamiltonian, which is studied in the companion paper
[
41
]
, for all
\kappa\in[0,1/2]
and some
r>0
depending only on
\kappa
and the interaction strength
\psi.

Another natural choice of
N_{\mathcal{A}}
is given by
N_{\mathcal{A}}=\widetilde{H}
where
\widetilde{H}=H+h_{0}+1
and
H\geq-h_{0}.
For this choice (
4.13
) is trivially satisfied for
r=0.
The alternative range
0<\kappa\leq 1
therefore applies. On the other hand, conditions (
4.11
) and (
4.12
) are non-trivial and need to be verified explicitly in this case.

We now proceed to show that under conditions (
4.11
) and (
4.13
), the generators
\mathcal{L}^{\leq M}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
are close. For this, we first prove the following technical Lemma. In what follows, we denote for some function
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle L^{\alpha}:=\int f(s)e^{isH}A^{\alpha}e^{-isH}ds,\qquad L^{\alpha,\leq M}

\displaystyle:=\int f(s)e^{isH}(A^{\alpha})^{\leq M}e^{-isH}ds.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
and set of bare jumps
\{A^{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}
and assume
Condition

A
and, for some self-adjoint and positive semidefinite
N_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\kappa\in(0,1/2),
(
4.11
) are satisfied and further that
e^{N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}A^{\alpha}e^{-2N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}},

e^{-N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}A^{\alpha}
and
A^{\alpha}e^{-N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}
are bounded.
Furthermore, assume that (
4.13
) holds for
r\geq 0
and
\kappa\in(0,1/2)
as above and that

(4.14)

\displaystyle C_{f}:=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{r|t|^{2\kappa}}|f(t)|dt<\infty.

Then for
M\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\displaystyle\|(L^{\alpha}\!-\!L^{\alpha,\leq M})e^{-N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}\|\!\leq\!C_{f}\,q(M)e^{-M^{\kappa}},

\displaystyle\|((L^{\alpha})^{\dagger}L^{\alpha}-\left(L^{\alpha,\leq M}\right)^{\dagger}L^{\alpha,\leq M})e^{-2N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}\|\leq C^{2}_{f}C_{A}\,q(M)e^{-M^{\kappa}},\,

where we denoted
C_{A}:=\max_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\left(\|e^{N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}A^{\alpha}e^{-2N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}\|+\|A^{\alpha}e^{-N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}\|+\|e^{-N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}A^{\alpha}\|\right)+2Q<\infty
,
Q:=\sup_{M\geq 1}q(M)e^{-M^{\kappa}}<\infty
.

Proof.

We use (
4.11
) together with (
4.13
) to see for
\ket{\psi}\in D\left(e^{N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}\right)
that we have

\displaystyle\|(L^{\alpha}-L^{\alpha,\leq M})\ket{\psi}\|

\displaystyle\leq\int|f(s)|\|(A^{\alpha}-(A^{\alpha})^{\leq M})e^{-isH}\ket{\psi}\|\,ds

\displaystyle\leq\int|f(s)|\left\|(A^{\alpha}-(A^{\alpha})^{\leq M})e^{-N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}\right\|\left\|e^{N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}e^{-isH}\ket{\psi}\right\|ds

\displaystyle\leq\int|f(s)|e^{\tfrac{r|s|^{2\kappa}}{2}}\left\|(A^{\alpha}-(A^{\alpha})^{\leq M})e^{-N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}\right\|\left\|e^{N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}\ket{\psi}\right\|ds

(4.15)

\displaystyle\leq C_{f}\,q(M)e^{-M^{\kappa}}\left\|e^{N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}\ket{\psi}\right\|,

which shows the first inequality in Lemma
4.7
.

Next, we control squares of the
L
operators for
\ket{\varphi}\in D(e^{2N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}})
:

\displaystyle\|((L^{\alpha})^{\dagger}L^{\alpha}-\left(L^{\alpha,\leq M}\right)^{\dagger}L^{\alpha,\leq M})\ket{\varphi}\|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\|(L^{\alpha})^{\dagger}(L^{\alpha}-L^{\alpha,\leq M})\ket{\varphi}\|

(4.16)

\displaystyle\qquad+\|((L^{\alpha})^{\dagger}-\left(L^{\alpha,\leq M}\right)^{\dagger})L^{\alpha,\leq M}\ket{\varphi}\|.

The first term above can be controlled by realizing that by assumption we have for
\ket{\psi}\in D(e^{N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}})
that
\ket{\psi}\in D((L^{\alpha})^{\dagger})
with

\displaystyle\|(L^{\alpha})^{\dagger}\ket{\psi}\|

\displaystyle\leq\int|f(s)|\,\|(A^{\alpha})^{\dagger}e^{-isH}\ket{\psi}\|\,ds\leq\int|f(s)|\,\|(A^{\alpha})^{\dagger}e^{-N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}\|\|e^{N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}e^{-isH}\ket{\psi}\|\,ds

(4.17)

\displaystyle\leq C_{f}\|e^{-N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}A^{\alpha}\|\|e^{N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}\ket{\psi}\|.

Hence, combining with an analogous argument to (
4.15
), we get
for
\ket{\varphi}\in D(e^{2N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}})

(4.18)

\displaystyle\|(L^{\alpha})^{\dagger}\,(L^{\alpha}-L^{\alpha,\leq M})\ket{\varphi}\|\!\leq\!C^{2}_{f}\,q(M)e^{-M^{\kappa}}\left\|e^{-N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}A^{\alpha}\right\|\|e^{2N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}\ket{\varphi}\!\|.

To control the second term in (
4.16
), we note that for
\ket{\varphi}\in D(e^{2N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}})
we have

\displaystyle\left\|e^{N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}L^{\alpha,\leq M}\ket{\varphi}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\int|f(s)|e^{\frac{r|s|^{2\kappa}}{2}}\left\|e^{N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}e^{isH}(A^{\alpha})^{\leq M}e^{-2N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}\right\|ds\,\left\|e^{2N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}\ket{\varphi}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\int|f(s)|e^{r|s|^{2\kappa}}ds\,\left(\left\|e^{N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}A^{\alpha}e^{-2N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}\right\|+Q\right)\left\|e^{2N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}\ket{\varphi}\right\|

\displaystyle=C_{f}\left(\left\|e^{N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}A^{\alpha}e^{-2N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}\right\|+Q\right)\left\|e^{2N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}\ket{\varphi}\right\|,

From this
and (
4.15
) we bound the second term in (
4.16
) as

\displaystyle\|((L^{\alpha})^{\dagger}-\left(L^{\alpha,\leq M}\right)^{\dagger})L^{\alpha,\leq M}\ket{\varphi}\|\leq C^{2}_{f}\,q(M)e^{-M^{\kappa}}\left(\left\|e^{N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}A^{\alpha}e^{-2N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}\right\|+Q\right)\left\|e^{2N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}\ket{\varphi}\right\|,

which finishes the proof.

∎

Next, we show that the generators
\mathcal{L}^{\leq M}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}
are close when evaluated on states
\rho
satisfying the superpolynomial energy constraint

\displaystyle\operatorname{Tr}\left(e^{4N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}\rho\right)<\infty.

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
and set of bare jumps
\{A^{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}
and assume
Condition

A
and, for some self-adjoint and positive semidefinite
N_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\kappa\in(0,1/2),
(
4.11
) are satisfied and further that
e^{N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}A^{\alpha}e^{-2N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}},

e^{-N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}A^{\alpha}
and
A^{\alpha}e^{-N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}
are bounded. Furthermore, assume that (
4.13
) holds for
r\geq 0
and
\kappa
as above and that

\displaystyle C_{f}:=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{r|t|^{2\kappa}}|f(t)|dt<\infty.

Then for all
\beta,\,\sigma_{E}>0,

M\in\mathbb{N}
and states
\rho
satisfying
E_{k}:=\operatorname{Tr}(e^{kN_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}\rho)<\infty
,
k\in\{2,4\}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|(\mathcal{L}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H}-\mathcal{L}^{\leq M}_{\sigma_{E},\widehat{f},H})(\rho)\right\|_{1}

(4.19)

\displaystyle\quad\leq C_{\beta,r,\kappa}C^{2}_{f}\,C_{A}\,|\mathcal{A}|\,q(M)e^{-M^{\kappa}}\left(\sigma_{E}\exp\!\left(\tfrac{\pi^{2}}{8\beta^{2}\sigma_{E}^{2}}\right)\sqrt{E_{4}}+\exp\left(r+\tfrac{r^{2}}{8\sigma^{2}_{E}}\right)\,E_{2}\right),

for some constant
C_{\beta,r,\kappa}\geq 0
and with
C_{A}
being defined in Lemma
4.7
.

Proof.

We make use of the integral representation of Proposition
4.4
. First, we denote the operator

\displaystyle G^{\leq M}_{\sigma_{E}}\!:=\!-\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\!\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\!\!\!\!\!g(t)e^{itH}(L^{\alpha,\leq M})^{\dagger}L^{\alpha,\leq M}e^{-itH}\!dt.

Denoting
\Delta(L^{\alpha})^{2}:=(L^{\alpha,\leq M})^{\dagger}L^{\alpha,\leq M}-(L^{\alpha})^{\dagger}L^{\alpha},
we directly obtain from Lemma
4.7
that for any state
\rho
for which
\operatorname{Tr}(e^{4N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}\rho)\leq E_{4}<\infty
we have

\displaystyle\|(G^{\leq M}_{\sigma_{E}}-G_{\sigma_{E}})\rho\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\int|g(t)|\,\|\Delta(L^{\alpha})^{2}e^{-itH}\rho\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\|\Delta(L^{\alpha})^{2}e^{-2N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}\|\int|g(t)|\left\|e^{2N_{\mathcal{A}}^{\kappa}}e^{-itH}\rho\right\|_{1}dt

\displaystyle\leq C^{2}_{f}C_{A}\,|\mathcal{A}|q(M)e^{-M^{\kappa}}\sqrt{E_{4}}\int|g(t)|e^{r|t|^{2\kappa}/2}dt

\displaystyle\leq C_{\beta,r,\kappa}C^{2}_{f}C_{A}\,|\mathcal{A}|\,\sigma_{E}\exp\!\left(\tfrac{\pi^{2}}{8\beta^{2}\sigma_{E}^{2}}\right)q(M)e^{-M^{\kappa}}\sqrt{E_{4}},

where in the last inequality we have used
7
7

7

Recall the definition
g(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-\nu^{2}/8\sigma_{E}^{2}}}{1+e^{\beta\nu/2}}e^{-i\nu t}d\nu
, and note
\tfrac{e^{-\nu^{2}/8\sigma_{E}^{2}}}{1+e^{\beta\nu/2}}
is analytic for
|\Im{\nu}|<\frac{2\pi}{\beta}
. Hence, by shifting the Contour of integration in the definition of
g(t)
to
\nu\mapsto\nu-i\tfrac{\pi}{\beta}
for
t>0
and to
